ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u1	The meeting was called to order. Appearance of the President of the Government to the plenary session of the House to report on the European Council of 25 and 26 June 2015. The President of the Government has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u2	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I appear before the plenary session of the House to report on the content of the European Council held in Brussels on 25 and 26 June. That is what is reflected on the agenda of this extraordinary session of the Congress, but as there have been up to four meetings of Heads of State and Government of the euro area or of the European Union as a whole dealing with the Greek crisis, I will also refer, if you will allow me, to the development of the negotiations and the agreement - which we hope will be final - that was reached last weekend. Let us begin with what was discussed in the Council on 25 and 26 June last, and we will do so with a strong recognition. The European Council expressed its gratitude to Jacques Delors, former President of the European Commission, for his outstanding contribution to the development of the European project and decided to grant him the title of a European citizen of honour. Our saying is that it is a good thing to be grateful and the Europeans in general, and the Spanish in particular, we owe thanks to Jacques Delors. So far there were only two people with the title of honorary citizen of Europe: Jean Monnet, one of the founding fathers of what is now the European Union, and Helmut Kohl, the former Chancellor of the Federal Republic of Germany, of whom it is not necessary to insist on their values as worthy of this recognition. Now he joins them Jacques Delors, one of those people who can be said to have left an indelible mark. Your contributions as President of the European Commission, on such important issues as the creation of the internal market and even the birth of our common currency, point to a before and after in the history of the Union. A few days ago we celebrated the 30th anniversary of the signing of Spain's agreement to enter the European Community. Delors was then the President of the Commission. Throughout his career he proved to be a good friend of Spain and the Spanish. Ladies and gentlemen, let us now turn to the matters before the Council. Much of the work involved the Mediterranean migration crisis on the basis of the principles agreed at the extraordinary European Council on 23 April and the recent European migration agenda presented by the Commission. It is absolutely necessary to stress - and I did so to my fellow Europeans - that the shipwrecks and deaths that have occurred in the Mediterranean are a real human tragedy, to which the European Union must respond in solidarity on the basis of the principles of solidarity and shared responsibility with a priority objective: to prevent the recurrence of tragedies such as those that have occurred in the Mediterranean. The Union must be fully involved in managing this crisis, and it must do so with ambition, common sense and maximum effectiveness. The opposite would be unacceptable and would undermine the founding spirit of this common project, which we call the European Union. During the meeting, and in response to the request made to me by several members of the European Council, I had the opportunity to present Spanish experience in the management of migratory flows. This experience was seen as a model to be followed by the whole of Europe. Agreements to establish labour migration channels have been vigorously promoted. High-level contacts and ongoing dialogue with the authorities of the countries of origin of migration flows have been established. Young people's training and employment programmes, which were the main candidates for irregular immigration, had been promoted and whose flight, owing to lack of opportunities, deprived their countries of development opportunities. Instruments of migration cooperation were concluded, including a large number of readmission agreements. Both the Frontex Agency's participation and the funding of European programmes dedicated to combating irregular immigration have increased, and multilateral forums for dialogue and cooperation have been developed, such as the Rabat Process, which aims to promote the organisation of legal migration, the fight against irregular migration and the relationship between migration and development. In the light of this experience, the European Union is committed to putting forward proposals and initiatives to promote cooperation with countries of origin, strengthen the role of Frontex and develop a relocation and resettlement system for people in need of international protection. This is a matter that has been dealt with for a long time. The European Council of 24 and 25 October 2013 addressed this issue after the Lampedusa shipwrecks, which took place on 3 April 2013. I then had the opportunity to present to the Council a number of proposals to make the European Union much more active in this area. Subsequently, on 23 April this year, an extraordinary European Council met to address the humanitarian tragedy in the Southern Mediterranean off the coast of Libya. From all the experiences received and from the implementation of the agreements adopted so far, a series of immediate reaction measures, such as the relocation from Italy and Greece to other States of the European Union, on a temporary and exceptional basis, of 40,000 people from Syria and Eritrea in clear need of international protection, are born as resolutions of this Council. The Council will adopt by consensus this same month of July a formula for such relocation. It has also been decided to grant additional emergency financial assistance to the most affected Member States and the resettlement of 20,000 people, who are also in need of international protection, from third countries to the European Union. Once again, I have transferred to the Council that Spain's involvement and support will always be available to curb, as a matter of urgency, this intolerable human bleeding and that it is a European problem that requires a European solution. Ladies and gentlemen, let us now turn to the conclusions of the European Council contained in the second item on security and defence issues, a debate in which the Secretary-General of NATO has also taken part. We have agreed to push forward the new internal security strategy in the revision of the overall foreign and security policy strategy. Both must be closely coordinated, as security is indivisible. In addition, we have stressed the need to move forward so that the European Union budget ensures adequate funding for this chapter, which will enable us to strengthen our capacities in preventing and effectively managing the new forms of the crisis we are facing. What we have learned in recent years is that internal security and external security are closely interrelated and that all the countries of the European Union are clear targets of terrorist barbarism. And one consequence: in the face of this threat it would be suicidal to claim a separate defence from each of our countries. Ladies and gentlemen, the third part of the Council's conclusions deals with economic issues under the heading of employment, growth and competitiveness. In this area, we have dealt with three issues: firstly, we have supported the specific recommendations to the Member States within the framework of the European Semester. Secondly, the Presidents of the Commission, the European Council, the Eurogroup, the European Central Bank and the European Parliament have presented us with their report on proposals to complete the design of economic and monetary union. Thirdly, we have dealt with the European strategy for the development of the digital economy. I shall refer briefly to each of these matters. European Semester. With regard to the European Semester, as you know, the June European Councils discuss the specific recommendations addressed to the Member States. This is the last step in the process of economic policy coordination during the six-month period. The recommendations addressed to Spain this year reflect that our country's situation has changed radically, and that within the euro area it is the one that grows the most and the one that creates the most net employment. For example, only a couple of years ago Spain was on the verge of opening a sanctioning procedure for the very serious economic imbalances accumulated. Today, however, Spain has only four recommendations compared to nine in 2013 and eight in 2014. The Commission considers that our imbalances are no longer excessive. This is the balance sheet of an intense process of reforms and a great effort for the whole of Spanish society. As regards the report on the future of economic and monetary union, drawn up by the group of so-called five presidents, Spain has been very involved in its preparation, when it presented proposals that are both ambitious and concrete. Monetary union must make progress in its integration in order for the euro to function properly. We must therefore push forward a long-term process that will lead us to a greater degree of convergence of our national economies while creating the necessary corrective mechanisms in the face of different cyclical situations between Member States. In this respect, progress needs to be made in five scenarios: a more integrated internal market in areas such as energy, retail banking or the digital agenda, among many others; in labour mobility it is important, in particular, to ensure the portability of workers' rights, such as unemployment benefits, throughout the Union, and also to ensure full recognition of professional qualifications and qualifications; coordination of economic policies to prevent fiscal and external imbalances from accumulating which put the euro at risk and to this end, new competitiveness indicators are needed, as they existed in the Maastricht process, allowing Member States to implement economic policies compatible with the single currency; in the fiscal union with the creation, sooner rather than later, of a budget for the whole euro area, and greater political union to ensure that the governance system agreed for the euro area respects democratic legitimacy in decision-making. In order to advance this integration Spain defends a gradual but continuous process based on convergence criteria. Well, many of the ideas put forward by the Spanish Government have been reflected in the report of the five presidents, so I can only express my satisfaction. For example, the report advocates the establishment of a gradual convergence process based on indicators. This process would lead to greater fiscal integration and the creation of fiscal capacity for the euro area. The five presidents consider it necessary to strengthen the monitoring of competitiveness as an essential element to ensure the stability of the euro and recognise the importance of further progress in the internal market and in labour mobility. The challenge now is that this report by the five presidents can be agreed as soon as possible so that its measures can be implemented immediately. Within this economic chapter I will also refer, as I pointed out earlier, to the Digital Agenda. We believe that this is a key area of action to ensure economic growth, employment and innovation as a productive model that takes full advantage of the opportunities offered by digital technologies. We are among the countries that defend long-term ambition and vision for this agenda, and in order to benefit fully from this technological revolution Europe needs, in order to cope with market fragmentation, to build support infrastructures and to contribute to the digitisation of industry. In this, as in the rest of the Europe 2020 initiatives, both the European and national administrations must coordinate their efforts to achieve the necessary synergies. The Internet and communication and information technologies are now reaching the entire economy. The global economy is increasingly a digital economy and the digital economy can be an important driver of growth, innovation and quality employment. In this new reality, the European Union cannot stay behind. It must strategically position itself and promote wealth creation at all levels of the digital value chain, from the content industry to the development of digital platforms and the deployment of networks. To this end, we must make progress in integrating the European digital market, while ensuring that consumer rights are protected and that content creators are adequately remunerated. Thus, in this European Council we have committed ourselves to progress in various areas of the Digital Agenda, as reflected in the conclusions. In particular, the European Union will take measures to remove barriers to trade in on-line goods and services, with the aim of ensuring that consumers have equal access to on-line goods and services sold in another Member State. Secondly, ensuring portability and facilitating cross-border access to content, ensuring high protection of intellectual property rights - this was included in the conclusions proposed by Spain -. In addition, in this area the European Union will ensure the protection of cultural diversity. Thirdly, to promote the development of digital skills among citizens. It is essential that citizens have the necessary training and tools to make the most of the advantages offered by the digital economy - this was also introduced in the conclusions proposed by Spain -. Fourthly, progress in standardisation; it is about Europe developing its own standards, those that best fit our market and industry, an issue that we discussed at the October 2013 European Council. The European Union then decided to give impetus to the digital market and to the development of the single market in telecommunications. This commitment has been embodied in a new regulation, by which important steps are taken. Firstly, roaming charges are eliminated from June 2017, i.e. European citizens will no longer have to pay a surcharge for their calls or messages when travelling within the European Union. Already since April 2016 the rates will be cheaper, by introducing the regulation limits to surcharges both for voice calls and for SMS or data messages. In addition, the regulation introduces strong rules to ensure the neutrality of the network, i.e. to ensure that citizens can freely and under the same conditions access all content of the network, without the platforms being able to discriminate or prioritize content without just cause. Ladies and gentlemen, I will never insist on this matter enough. Unfortunately, in Europe we are lagging behind in this area and there is an urgent need to speed up the pace. Allow me to bring here a few words with which I was beginning to appear before this House in October 2013. They are as follows: Ladies and gentlemen, as you know, already in this year 2013 more than half of the books published in the United States do so not in paper media, but in digital media. It is an example, among many, of the effects of the so-called digital revolution and the best proof of that is that, as you will have read a few days ago in the press, three of the world's first four companies by stock market capitalization are digital companies: Apple, which occupies the first place; Google, the third, and Microsoft, the fourth. 40% of new jobs created in the United States are jobs in the digital economy. On the contrary, 300,000 job vacancies in this sector were left uncovered in the European Union in 2011 and, if this trend is not reversed by 2015 - it said in 2013 - there could be up to 900,000 uncovered job vacancies. Europe is not having the leadership it should aspire to in this digital economy. The main companies are American. Digital jobs are created primarily close to the decision-making centres of these large digital companies or in emerging economies with a trained digital talent base, such as India. It is clear that Europe has to gain digital prominence. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, let us now turn to the Greek matter. If we had to explain in very few words the situation that had been reached in the negotiations between the Greek Government and the European institutions and the rest of the States of the European Union, it would be sufficient to read the first paragraph of the Euro Summit Declaration of 12 July. It says: The Euro Summit underlines the crucial need to restore a relationship of trust with the Greek Government as a precondition for a possible future agreement on a new programme of the European Stability Mechanism, the MEDE, and in this context the assumption of responsibilities by the Greek Government is key, and successful implementation will be a consequence of political commitments. Restoring trust or otherwise, returning to a previous situation that had been lost: mutual trust between the Greek Government, the institutions and the rest of the countries that make up the economic and monetary union. Overcoming the mistrust generated in recent times is the challenge we are trying to overcome. This was agreed unanimously. Last Sunday's Council set out a clear and precise road map to guide it out of the maze that had been reached in the negotiations. It is important to highlight this. A clear process has been defined, with specific content and time targets for verification. It is not an agreement of intent, it is a catalogue of concrete measures. As far as our position is concerned, Spain has always had a constructive attitude and, at all times, has been in favour of maintaining Europe's support for Greece; it is not something new. We have been in that position for more than five years, but, yes, in a framework of mutual loyalty. European aid demands the Greek Government's responsibility as a counterpart and there can be no doubt as to its willingness to fulfil its commitments. It is not necessary to detail the latest events that you all know, but the result of what has happened is as follows: First, the economic situation in Greece has deteriorated very rapidly, in a matter of months; it has gone from a growth forecast of almost 3% for this year to a decrease of between at least 2 and at least 4; it has also deteriorated the situation of the banks, not to mention the problems that the citizens of Greece are having, who are victims of a corralite and do not have free access to their money. Secondly, the unilateral actions of the Greek Government have also led to a huge deterioration in the confidence of its partners, and it is absolutely essential to rebuild that basis of political confidence. We must act on three objectives: to meet the urgent financing needs of the Greek economy, to lay the foundations for overcoming this situation as soon as possible and to return to scenarios of growth and job creation, and to restore confidence in the Greek Government's commitment to the euro and to the rules of the single currency. We have seen - we shall see what happens next - a radical change in the attitude of the Greek Government, without which no agreement would have been possible. This change opens up the possibility of negotiating a new bailout whose amount has increased significantly due to the damage suffered by the Greek financial system during these weeks of restrictions. In any event, the possibility of negotiating a third aid programme is open provided that Greece offers a very firm commitment to comply with the agreement. Ladies and gentlemen, over the last three weeks we have had four summits to discuss the situation in Greece: the extraordinary Euro Summit of Monday 22 June; the European Council of 25 and 26 June; the extraordinary Euro Summit of Tuesday 7 July; and the extraordinary Euro Summit of Sunday 12 July. Four summits in three weeks, with the calendar subjecting us all to insufferable pressure; and five months the time running from the end of January to the end of June in which it is difficult to qualify the negotiating strategy of the Greek Government. Although it is almost telegraphic, it is worth recalling the substantial aspects of this negotiating process. Before the January 2015 elections, Greece had an existing financial assistance programme that remained to be paid out of €7.2 billion and met some of its conditionality, i.e. those measures that countries put in place in return for financial assistance. These measures are aimed at channelling economic policy and placing the country on the path of growth and in a situation where it can be financed on a regular basis by issuing debt on the markets. Support for Greece had allowed the Greek economy to grow again – already in 2014 – and its forecasts for 2015 were close to 3% economic growth, as I pointed out earlier. Syriza's arrival in power marked a radical change in the situation. The new Government proposed not only non-compliance with the current programme, but to reverse many of the reforms carried out. The uncertainty created paralyzed economic activity and massive outflows of deposits from Greek banks began to be recorded. This shows the uneasiness of many Greek citizens with the new situation. From the outset, the position of all euro countries has been clear. Europe has always been ready to support Greece, but within the Community law that we must all comply with and within a framework compatible with the economic policy decisions of monetary union. This has, of course, also been the position of the Government I chair. Thus, as early as February, the Eurogroup reached a minimum agreement: the Greek Government had to present alternative measures with similar effects to the current programme and, in return, the outstanding EUR 7.2 billion would be disbursed, and a four-month extension was agreed to end on 30 June. On the basis of this - I insist - minimum commitment, the European Central Bank has been supporting Greek banks. The Central Bank activated its emergency liquidity line for Greece; this line, which can only be used in the event of temporary liquidity difficulties, has avoided the bankruptcy of Greek banks and the loss of savings and deposits of many Greek citizens. Therefore, as early as February, the scenario was clear: the euro area offered support and flexibility; in return, Greece was asked to respect the rules and to launch a solid reform programme that would serve to correct the country's imbalances and generate sustainable growth that would allow it to refinance itself normally in the markets. In four months, however, the Greek Government did not submit proposals for reform which, within the framework of the flexibility of the February agreement, would enable it to be complied with and ensure the disbursement of money. We then had an extraordinary first Euro Summit, on Monday 22 June, in which there was a more constructive attitude by the Greek Government. Negotiations continued in those late June, however, and despite progress and close positions, on the morning of 27 June the Greek Government agreed to convene a referendum. All political forces of the opposition, except the neo-fascist Golden Dawn, voted against this call in the Greek Parliament. It is still unclear what the issue was before the Greek people; what the Government of Tsipras submitted to it were documents of a negotiation still under way and which also corresponded to a programme which, by decision of the Greek Government, had been allowed to expire. In view of the lack of commitments by the Greek authorities, the financial aid programme expired on 30 June without an agreement having been reached. Without the protective umbrella of an aid programme and with ever-increasing outflows of deposits, the Greek Government had to decree the closure of the banks and impose a corralite. The citizens saw their cash availability restricted to 60 euros a day and Greek retirees had to queue up to collect part of their pension, even with the support of the European Central Bank. If the European Central Bank had withdrawn its emergency line, the Greek banks would have broken down. The result of all this has been very negative for Greek citizens. From an expected growth of around 3 % in 2015 to a clear deterioration in the economic situation in Greece, as I pointed out earlier, from an additional 3 % to an increase of between less than 2 % and less than 4 % in 2015. In these certainly complex circumstances, because we did not use another expression, we met again at the extraordinary summit on 7 July. The steps to follow were then agreed upon. After the end of the second programme, the maintenance of European aid will require the granting of a new programme; a third programme which Greece had rejected, as you all know, previously; a third MEDE financial aid programme, in accordance with the rules of this institution. On 8 July the Greek Government submitted to the MEDE an official request for a third programme and on 9 July a detailed proposal for reforms to be implemented in order to obtain funding. This is a three-year macroeconomic adjustment programme with specific conditionality. The Greek request already included a detail of measures to be implemented immediately, very similar to the proposals being discussed before the referendum was called. This proposal was supported by the majority of the Greek Parliament, with the support of the main opposition parties: New Democracy, To Potami, Pasok and Anel, despite the vote against seventeen Syriza deputies. They also protested against the Golden Dawn Party and the Greek Communist Party. From here, as set out by the MEDE Treaty, the following is necessary for the adoption of a programme: detailed assessment by the institutions of the country's financing needs, the sustainability of its debt and the risks to the financial stability of the euro area and a unanimous agreement by the Eurogroup to start negotiations. Once this agreement has been reached, a detailed memorandum of understanding - MOU - of specific conditionality must be negotiated and agreed by the Eurogroup for the final approval of the programme. In addition, there are six countries that need the approval of their parliaments in order to carry out these procedures: Finland, Austria, Germany, the Netherlands, Estonia and Slovakia. Ladies and gentlemen, I announce to you that, although this procedure is not compulsory in our country, it is my intention to bring the Spanish position on the programme to Parliament for debate and, if necessary, to this House, because there are many resources that are being demanded to guarantee Spanish taxpayers. [[Applauds]] I am already telling you that I am in the best position to bring this matter to an end in the best possible way. Ladies and gentlemen, the evaluation carried out by the institutions shows how much worse the situation has worsened in these months. Public debt, which was sustainable at the end of 2014, is now unsustainable, and this is due to the relaxation of policies in recent months, which have led to the growing economic deterioration. Banks now need 25 billion euros to keep running. In addition to the strong outflows of deposits, there was an increase in outstanding or doubtful claims; doubtful claims reached 36 % of the total by the end of the first quarter of 2015 and defaults were already 8 %. For all the above, Greece's funding needs have increased significantly. The institutions estimate that a new programme of between €82 billion and €86 billion is now needed. Before the expiry of the second programme and the closure of banks, the estimated financing needs of the International Monetary Fund were EUR 52 billion. We thus arrived at the Eurogroup on Saturday 11 July and at the Eurosummary this Sunday. As I have already said, the position of the Spanish Government has not changed; we remain ready to support the Greek people within the European rules. This is also the position of the rest of the euro area partners, but the Greek Government has caused a breach of confidence and the first thing it has to do is to regain the confidence of the rest of the European partners. That's essential. We have lent Greece a lot over the past five years and this has been a major effort, especially for countries that, like Spain, were not experiencing an easy economic situation. Ladies and gentlemen, Greece has already received more than EUR 200 billion in loans on very favourable terms - this is more than its GDP - and now it needs EUR 80 billion more. Support has not only been through loans. Already in 2012, the euro countries adopted restructuring measures that eased the burden of Greek public debt. This included an extension of time limits, interest rate cuts and a 50% write-off of the debt in the hands of private creditors, which resulted in a reduction of EUR 100 billion in debt to Greece. Greece was granted a lack of interest payments of 10 years and the principal of 30 years. Moreover, then, we European partners commit ourselves to taking further measures if necessary, as long as Greece also fulfils its reform commitments. The euro area remains ready to honour this commitment as long as Greece implements the MOU measures still to be agreed and not before the first revision is successfully completed. In the Sunday agreement, nominal debt write-offs are completely discarded. Ladies and gentlemen, at the 12 July summit we agreed that Greece will immediately legislate a number of reform measures in line with what was offered in its letter of request for a programme on 8 July. These measures include the following. Today: VAT reform, reform of the pension system to make it sustainable, full implementation of the European commitments on budgetary stability and the fiscal pact; Greece will also adopt a quasi-automatic rule on spending cuts in case of deviations from budgetary objectives. Before 22 July: reform of the judicial system to speed up procedures and reduce costs and full implementation of measures taken at European level to regulate bank resolutions and restructuring. Once the first reforms, i.e. today's reforms, have been approved by the Greek Parliament, the Eurogroup will be ready to agree on the start of negotiations for the third programme. This third programme will involve the International Monetary Fund, which has been a condition in all the macroeconomic adjustment programmes approved by the MED. Thus, the Greek Government is committed to formally requesting support from the International Monetary Fund from March 2016, when the current programme with the institution expires. With regard to the conditionality of this new programme, account must be taken of the deterioration of the situation in recent months and the increased funding needs. This implies a need for strong reform commitments by the Greek authorities in key areas for the success of an adjustment programme, i.e. pension system, product markets, energy system management, labour market and financial sector. The ultimate goal is to bring Greece back to a path of sustainable growth and get it to regain market access as soon as possible. In addition, the Greek authorities have committed themselves to speeding up the implementation of the privatisation programme. As part of the agreement, EUR 50 billion of Greek assets will be transferred to an independent fund. The sale of these assets will generate resources to deal with bank recapitalisations, debt reduction and finance an investment programme of €12.5 billion. Working methods with the institutions will also be standardised in accordance with the rules of the European Stability Mechanism. In the event of a financial assistance programme, the Greek Government shall consult with the institutions any legislative initiative in the areas relevant to the success of the programme. As part of this commitment, the Greek Government will reverse those reforms introduced in recent months that run counter to the objectives of the programme or adopt equivalent measures. Except for the Humanitarian Crisis Act, which was adopted at the time by the Greek Parliament. Ladies and gentlemen, all this must serve to restore confidence between the Greek Government and its partners in the Eurogroup and to lay the foundations for a new programme that will definitively lead the Greek economy and enable it to resume the path of growth that has now been interrupted. Having said all this, the euro countries are aware that Greece has immediate financing needs for now; it has to deal with major payments with the European Central Bank in July, next Monday, and in August, and it has to resolve its arrears with the International Monetary Fund and deal with the situation of banks after more than two weeks of closure and write-off. The solution to this problem is not easy. There's little time. The first payment, as I have just pointed out, is to the European Central Bank for more than EUR 4 billion by 20 July, and Greece also has a non-payment to the Monetary Fund for EUR 1.6 billion. Once the Greek Government has allowed the second programme to expire, there are no mechanisms in place to mobilize the necessary resources rapidly. That is why we have agreed in this Euro Summit to give the finance ministers a mandate to discuss these issues as a matter of urgency. I would also point out that the Commission is committed to working with the Greek authorities to mobilise €35 billion over the next three to five years in support of investment and economic activity. As part of this support package, exceptional measures will be taken to enable Greece to make better use of the resources allocated to it from the Structural Funds. Greece also has opportunities for investment financing under the Juncker Plan. In conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, we have laid the foundations for a solid and lasting agreement to support Greece in this Euro Summit. It is now up to the Greek Government to fulfil its commitments and to regain the confidence of the European partners. In recent months a great deal of time has been wasted and the economic situation in Greece has deteriorated considerably. The most harmed in the last instance has been the Greek people. I now believe that it is time for the Greek Government to show wisdom and responsibility at a time. Ladies and gentlemen, this has been a sad story that can end up better than we could have imagined just a few days ago. It has not been miraculously, but by the recovery of wisdom and the recognition of the most obvious reality. It will be difficult to return to the lost levels of confidence, but it can be achieved. It will be more difficult to avoid the frustration of a part of the Greek people, who are induced to reject some proposals just over a week ago, is now forced to accept others more demanding because the situation is much more serious. It'll be hard but you can get over it. What I am sure is that the euro, our common currency and the best symbol of an ongoing project will emerge from this test. It will not be the first time in the process of European integration. If in 1993 the European monetary system entered into crisis, then the reaction to create the euro arose; if between 2010 and 2012 we suffered from the sovereign debts of the European periphery, the reaction was to increase the ECB's powers and the development of the banking union. We've already done it and we'll do it again. We will achieve new political commitments that will allow us to achieve new levels of economic, social and political integration, and that will be, have no doubt, good for Europe and good for the citizens who inhabit it. Thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen. [[Applauds]]
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u3	Thank you very much, Mr President. Interventions by parliamentary groups. Firstly, Mr Sánchez has the floor for the Socialist Group.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u4	Thank you, Mr. President. Mr Rajoy, after listening to you, believe me, with attention, I am certain that you have been a stone guest to a crisis in which Spain played a lot. Your appearance, ladies and gentlemen, Mr Rajoy, is preceded by a black week for the Government in Europe; five days, five facts demonstrating the absence of Spain in the major European debates, which prove the failure, if not the isolation, of the Government of a Spain which in the past was a heavy burden of the Union and which today is only a light burden. [[Rumors]] The first of these events, to which you did not refer in your speech, was Mr De Guindos' bad candidacy to chair the Eurogroup. It was not enough, ladies and gentlemen, to be more papist than the Pope; it was not enough the support of the greatest defender of the grexit, that is, of the German finance minister, Schäuble; it was not enough the Spanish complicit silence to the most reactionary theses of the European Union, which advocated the expulsion of a Member State, that is, Greece, victim of of course of its mistakes, of the errors of its government, but also of the austerity imposed by Europe; it was not enough and it proved to be an insufficient price to obtain the presidency of the Eurogroup. On the contrary, ladies and gentlemen, today we know that Spain will pay a high cost for its institutional absence. The second of these events, ladies and gentlemen, as happened in Greece, has been to know that the Government of the Valencian Community, led by the one to whom the President of the Government promised never to abandon and always to be behind him, in front of him, lied in the public accounts. Ladies and gentlemen, the Valencian People's Party lies will cost Spanish taxpayers EUR 19 million. Who, Mr Rajoy, assumes the political responsibility for this deception? Before I presume, I believe that the Government of Spain should be humble enough to apologize to this new known deception. [[Applauds]] Ladies and gentlemen, the third of these events known this week to the Spanish Government in Europe has been the shameful use that the People's Party has made of the Greek drama. Ladies and gentlemen, the Spaniards have witnessed in recent weeks the lack of scruples of a government president in the face of Greek suffering. In his despair, Mr Rajoy believed that the Greek corralite could vote for him, and with that the only thing he has shown in the eyes of all the Spaniards has been his low political height at a historic moment in the construction of Europe. The fourth of these events, ladies and gentlemen, has been that while I had the opportunity to speak with the European Social Democratic leaders to defend the interests of Spain, which were only passing by to avoid the grexit, I could not speak with the President of the Government of Spain, of my own country, in order to be able to agree on a state position. I knew of your opinion, Mr Rajoy, for your rallies and for your false beatings, never for the direct dialogue that the leaders of this House deserve as representatives of national sovereignty. In the Greek crisis, Mr Rajoy, you have set an example of partyism, not patriotism. The fifth of these, Mr Rajoy, has been asylum and immigration policy. The position of the Government of Spain, ladies and gentlemen, to this fact reflects the genuine ingredients of its policy, which are the blockade, the insularity and the insensitivity, insensitivity to a tragedy that is pressing for an immediate response and once again blocking the solution that our European partners want to implement as soon as possible. Five days, Mr Rajoy, that marked your blackest week in Europe. Ladies and gentlemen, the decisions of the Blue Bank have made Spain absent from the Community economic institutions at a key historical moment. We have had a fighting and courageous people, but a small people, whose economy represented around 2% of the European Union's gross domestic product, who fought against a giant; a giant who crushed him, a giant who has managed to double his interests, a giant who has been able to double the interests of a government that was valiantly defending its people, but a giant made up of a handful of bureaucrats who did not seek the recovery of the Greek economy - or seek it - do not seek the welfare of the Greek people. The only thing they have sought has been humiliation and punishment of a rebel Government, which is the only thing they have sought with that pact. It is a pact that does not serve to recover the Greek economy, it serves nothing other than to give an exemplary punishment, as we said. Mr Rajoy, what a paradox, the same mistakes that gave rise to the origin of this European Union are repeated. You know, Mr Rajoy, that in 1919 the Versailles Pact was signed, which was the economic origin, unfortunately, of the Second World War. It is from the drama of the Second World War that the European Economic Community is fundamentally born from. That Versailles Pact served to humiliate the German people, but it did not serve to recover their economy, that Versailles Pact is being signed in the European Union, but this time against Greece and, what a paradox, also signed by Germany. That Versailles Pact gave rise to the germ of fascism, gave wings to Hitler, just as the pact that you have signed this weekend is to build a highway for the far right of France and build a highway for what you have rightly pointed out as the neofascism of Golden Dawn. They are slogans of bureaucrats found, designed in the laboratory of European social engineering that do not attend to the welfare or material conditions of Greek citizens, who will never understand as after six years, being prescribed a poison that has not recovered the economy, will insist on that path again. Another huge paradox, what is it? According to French intellectuals, the European Union was built to prevent the German empire from having the potential to rebuild itself. It was believed that the designs of the institutional networks of the European Union would serve to forget about the panzers and, what a paradox, in the end, what we have is a new construction of a German empire, but that on this occasion it does not use the panzers and the military route, but rather economic tools that also serve to make the governments and peoples of Europe claudiate to impose the measures and to make them a protectorate. That is the reality, and in that we agree with European leaders such as Mélenchon or Beiras, who speak of the new German empire; a new German empire that uses economic tools, no longer needs the military route to bend countries. A third paradox is that you and other European leaders are talking primarily about the rhetoric of preserving the social state. It is a lie, you have just signed the gravestone of the social conquests built by the anti-fascist generations and that in this country it was the generations that built around the transition the social state. You, by signing the pact with regard to Greece, are doing precisely what you are doing to end that process. They have not dealt with the structural and economic failures of the European Union, something you have not mentioned, in fact. Europe is a myth and it is for a double reason, not only because it is the Phoenician woman, seduced by Zeus turned into a bull, but because, when we talk about the European Union and talk about social conquests, with a model different from the Anglo-Saxon one, we lie. It is a myth because what is being supported is precisely the social regression and the finiteness of these social achievements characterized by public health, public education, public pensions and public services. That is what you have done in Greece, to promote the privatization of all the remaining public services. Sometimes we talk about the democratic deficit of the European Union, Mr Rajoy. What can be called a democratic deficit, it is that there is no democracy, it is that it shines for its absence! The European Union is a hydride, it is a monster with many heads, where it is not well known who makes the decisions, if Merkel does it, the Eurogroup does it, the European Commission does it or the European Central Bank does it. The real boss in this really existing European Union is the financial power, which uses puppets, bureaucrats, in the European Union to finally decide the concrete policies. You talked about the Greek corralite this morning here. Corralito is the answer, it is the effect of the policy of the European Central Bank, which is not ideologically neutral. The European Central Bank has an ideology whose policies affect the social majority. We are talking about a deeply neoliberal European Union, and not just because in 1983 Mitterrand had to make a neoliberal turn or in 1986, with the Single Act; or in 1992, with the Maastricht Treaty, to which our group opposed because we knew what was next, but because we know that this European Union has been designed to avoid any alternative to neoliberalism. It's like a straitjacket; don't tell us then that there's no alternative, say they're building a multi-headed monster to avoid alternatives to neoliberalism, to avoid people being able to decide democratically. There is much talk of democracy in the European Union, but what happens when a people votes against the wishes of the European elite? What happened to the failed European constitution when the Netherlands, France or Ireland voted not in the referendum? That the elite led by the European Union decided that the voters had been wrong, that they had voted badly and that the same content had to be included in the Treaty of Lisbon. They are the same reactions that you had the other day when in the referendum in Greece it was said no, and it was said no, not to the euro or the European Union, it was said no to the austerity that you were imposing. And you have not made any reference to these problems, nor have you made any reference to economic problems either, because it seems as if the cause of the Greek crisis or of the Spanish crisis really were caused by the citizens who wanted to have access to a home, who wanted to have a car to go to work with when it really had nothing to do with all that. Nor does it have anything to do with whether Mr De Guindos is head of the Eurogroup or not, because the choice was between death and death, and we want life. It may seem a contradiction, because Europe is the sum of the countries, but supranational and non-intergovernmental decisions would be required; supranationals are European and the others are the sum of the interests of all, which are sometimes contradictory and have not yet brought us to a good place. In all these weeks, two impulses have been seen here: the Greek Government, its leaders, wanting to raise the issue as a purely political problem and some European leaders, many, too many, wanting to raise it as a purely economic, not even economic issue. And it's not, because it's a political problem and it's an economic problem. I say that it will not solve the economic problem because the consequences in terms of well-being and inequality are and will be tremendous - then I will stop on this issue - and, with regard to the political problem, because uncertainty will not be reduced either, and I will explain why. Let us look at the new intergovernmental and not supranational basic agreement, as I said. For our group, it is substantially an agreement that insists on repeating the mistakes of the design of the past, of the design of the first programme for 2010, when the Greek debt was 130% of GDP, I think I recall, or of a figure of those characteristics, and of the design of the second plan for 2012, in which the strategy of the first one, which was already a failed plan, was emphasized. Despite the acknowledged failure of the 2010 plan, the same adjustment logic was imposed in the second rescue in 2012, and the same results were obtained. Why was it emphasized in 2012 in the same strategy that had already failed? I remember that the analysis that was made then - we also talked about it here in this House - was that the problem was not in the design of the plan - it was said - but in the lack of commitment on the part of Greece and in the failure to comply with the plan. Now, how is it possible to fulfill a plan when your economy is being devastated? I do not know whether or not Greece had the political will to comply when the situation was that of a devastated economy, what seems clear is that it did not have the opportunity to comply, from an economic point of view. Even if he had had all the will, the most wonderful institutions in the world and the most responsible politicians - which I doubt he would come together in all places and at the same time - yet Greece would not have been able to meet its commitments under that 2012 plan. Now we have a new deal. The new agreement reached on the morning of July 13 repeats, in our opinion, diagnosis, prescription and I fear what consequences of the past. Once again the mantra of non-restructuring of the debt/tax contraction is applied, yes, that so good - in quotes - has yielded results for Greece and for Europe. Moreover, quite perversely - I do believe that a corrective has been applied, if not punishment, to Greece; although it is said that no, we believe that a corrective has been applied, we will not call it punishment - nevertheless, it is insisted that the programme has failed only because of Greece. I have said this before, our group has no doubt that the new Greek Government is irresponsible, but we also have no doubt that a plan that cannot work does not work. The novelty of the plan is to introduce in this case two mechanisms to ensure - it is said - the Greek commitment: first, a parliamentary agreement; second, privatization of 50 billion euros of state assets, half of which will recapitalize the bank. With regard to the first, Mr Rajoy, you know that you have the value you have: none. Regarding the second, it seems like a joke because, how are the assets of the state valued? Selling state assets can take months, if not years. In the meantime, will the banks be recapitalized for that moment? In addition to practical impossibility, all this is quite obscene in political terms. The loans granted to Greece following the first two programmes, both in 2010 and 2012, amount to approximately 125 % of the Greek gross domestic product. That seems very generous if it were not because this money has mostly been spent on avoiding the non-payment of loans - as has been said here - both from the State and from the Greek banks to their foreign creditors, to which it has devoted itself. Only 11% of all this money has been spent on financing the services and investments of the Greek Government. Isn't this obscene, Mr. Rajoy? Only 11% to finance services and investments from the Greek Government. I think it would have been much more honest - let me tell you this - and better for the Greeks as a whole to have given that money directly to foreign banks. The Greeks, in any case, would not have received it, but at least the debt would not have increased, in addition to avoiding austerity and of course the precariousness of life for all citizens. That would have been quite embarrassing for all of us, but the result would have been generally better and also fairer. As I said, there is nothing left to eschew in Greece and that is why the new programme puts its eyes on public assets. The agreement also includes a recapitalisation of the European Financial Stability Mechanism of EUR 10 billion. This is the money that in the first instance will allow the Greeks to leave the corralito, it is said. This amount of departure is absolutely insufficient, as you and all of you very well know. Only between September 2014 and May 2015 came out of the Greek banks 33 billion euros, not counting the liquidity needs caused by the high ratio of loans unpaid by individuals or the fall in the value of sovereign debt. What, then, will the EUR 10 billion recapitalising the European Financial Stability Mechanism do? Not at all. This brief review of the situation, where we come from and where we are, may we ask ourselves what now, even if we put ourselves in positive terms. The new programme will add 25% more of GDP to Greek public debt. The conditions required of Greece will not be met, simply because they are impossible to meet. The programme will be reviewed if it is concluded that Greece has not fulfilled its commitments and financial aid will be suspended. Next is a new episode of grexit. This is the picture. In the meantime, Greek citizens will suffer even more, any hint of state welfare will disappear, public services will be further dismantled and, with this, any opportunity for social protection. The agreement, Mr Rajoy, ladies and gentlemen, leaves Greece, for the moment, within the euro, but its citizens outside Europe, far from the well-being and progress that the European project could once represent. In the medium term, they'll both be out if we don't do something different than what we're doing. That is why we ask ourselves: who benefits from this agony? Mr Rajoy, I believe that in this situation in which we are in any case, a European solidarity fund or mechanism should be set up with Greece, a mechanism managed by the Commission and aimed at directly granting social assistance which limits the impoverishment and exclusion of the weakest groups affected by the economic crisis and adjustment measures, such as children, unemployed young people, the long-term unemployed or families without income. I would insist that a European fund be managed directly by the Commission and intended to provide social assistance directly to Greek citizens. We also believe that it is important and necessary for the European Parliament to include regular political control clauses in the implementation of the rescue programme and its implementation by Greece. In short, President, after this black panorama, our group wants to insist on something that I have just said. If we do not do something of this which I am putting to you on behalf of my group - and if we do so, perhaps there is no solution - what we will have decided this weekend is that Greece remains, for the time being, within the euro but the Greeks leave the European Union, the Greeks leave Europe, and that would be a catastrophe not only for the Greeks but for the whole of Europe and for all those whom we consider ourselves, first of all, European citizens. ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u51	The Government's agreement that, in accordance with Article 15 of Organic Law 2/2012 of 27 April 2012 on budgetary stability and financial sustainability, the objectives of budgetary stability and public debt for all public administrations and each of their subsectors for the period 2016-2018 and the non-financial expenditure ceiling of the State budget for 2016 are set.
