ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u52	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the government is bringing to Congress today the agreement of the Council of Ministers setting the objectives of budgetary stability and public debt for all public administrations and for each of its subsectors for the period 2016-2018 and the non-financial spending limit of the state budget for 2016. By bringing this agreement to the House today, the Government fulfils its duty and the obligation it has assumed with all the Spaniards since the beginning of the legislature to ensure at all times the general interest, which in the case of Spain is to overcome definitively the economic crisis. That's what we committed ourselves to after winning the general elections in 2011 with broad citizen support. That has been what we have been doing over these three years and that is what we intend to do until the last day of our mandate, and nothing is going to divert us from that project and obviously from the path, the path, that we are developing. The objectives of budgetary stability lay down the limits of debt and public deficit to which all public administrations must move. It is part of the capacity, the power conferred on the Government by the Constitution, the coordination of the general economic policy of the country and, therefore, of all its public administrations, and the design of the framework within which both the autonomous communities and the local entities must act when drawing up their own budgets. The guidelines emanating from the Government must be followed by all, and this has allowed all the public administrations in these years to comply with the objectives committed, which has restored great credibility to our public accounts and made it possible for Spain to begin to overcome the crisis. On the other hand, as honourable Members know, the expenditure ceiling represents the front line for the next year's General State Budgets Bill. Our aim is to draw up, approve and process in Parliament for its final adoption budgets that provide the Spanish economy with the stability and credibility necessary to remain in 2016 on the same path as we are now going through. These are budgets which, like this year's budgets, serve the economic recovery, the growth of gross domestic product and, more importantly, the creation of employment. The Spanish economy - it is impossible not to recognize it - is increasingly at a better time and we cannot stop that process. The Spanish economy cannot afford to go on vacation, our economy needs to tell the world that it continues to move forward and that it does so with the same guidelines that have made it emerge with so much vitality in recent months, in recent years. It is our obligation to transfer citizens and businesses the certainty that economic recovery will follow, that we will also continue to reduce the public deficit and that the country will have budgets next year to move forward with the definitive overcoming of the crisis, so that economic activity continues to grow and that employment, above all, can grow ever more vigorously. The government's objective is that in the next parliamentary term we reach - as President Rajoy has already announced and reiterated on numerous occasions - the 20 million occupied, which we were already able to register in Spain at the beginning of this crisis in 2007. These 20 million occupied would make a large part of Spain in full employment and we are already very close to recovering the gross domestic product at the beginning of the economic crisis. With a growth like that which we now predict, next year, 2016, Spain will have a volume of economic activity very similar to that which we had before entering this economic crisis. Therefore, the Government's project is to draw up budgets thinking that it is best for the Spaniards and the best to continue along the path that has placed us as a benchmark of economic recovery in Europe. Ladies and gentlemen, the place that Spain occupies today in Europe and in the world has nothing to do with what we lived in just over three years ago. The current Spain and, above all, the one that is projected for the next few years does not look at all like Spain of 2011. We are much closer to being the country in which we all recognize ourselves; the country that entered the euro Europe with solvency, which helped to found it and which in the following years lived the economic process of greater progress and greater job creation in our entire history; a process that was interrupted by the financial crisis of 2007. Three years after seeing us on the verge of the rescue, on the verge of the financial collapse as a country, and three years after seeing how the treasure of Spanish society prevented the rescue and the financial collapse from being a reality, now - as I said before - Spain is becoming a reference from all over Europe. Our policies have put us at the forefront of growth among European partners, and even more importantly, in terms of job creation. According to the International Monetary Fund, our economy is going to grow the most this year among the world's major industrialized countries. Some of our European partners will grow, but they will grow less than half as much as we will. The OECD forecast is that, between 2015 and 2016, we will be at the forefront of job creation among all the member countries of the organisation, as we are already at the forefront of job creation in the euro area. Getting here hasn't been easy. Getting here has required efforts and sacrifices that are now getting their reward. Getting here has been possible thanks to a budgetary policy based on control, reduction of the public deficit and austerity in spending, parameters and assumptions that we are not going to abandon at this end of the legislature, because they are the parameters, premises and conditions that have allowed us to get here, overcoming and overcoming so many difficulties, and that is proving in practice to be the only solution to the economic crisis in our country. It is the budgetary policy that has been reinforced and flanked by the labour reform that has so much modernised collective bargaining in Spain, by the consolidation of the banking sector that today credit to small and medium-sized enterprises is a reality and, moreover, with increasingly accessible interest rates; with an energy reform that has made it possible for us today to lower the price of energy such as electricity, light consumption, which are absolutely essential to improve our competitiveness and our capacity for economic growth. Ladies and gentlemen, Spain is growing today and creating jobs because, during these three years, we have made a great effort to clean up the accounts of our public administrations. When at the beginning of the parliamentary term we were able to have full access to the financial situation of all our public administrations, we found that their total deficit was 9 %, rather than the 6 % which the previous socialist government had warned and committed. That was not a forecast failure; it was a failure of fiscal policy, of budgetary policy, which, in turn, had promoted the loss of revenue of around €70 billion and an excessive increase in expenditure, which led to the scenario of budgetary stability to which we were committed to Europe and, of course, to ourselves. That failure led to Spain's own economic activity and promoted the loss of 3.5 million jobs and also led to an increase in public debt by more than €30 billion. What did that increase in public debt do? It was buried in the tons of unproductive and inefficient spending and it was also accompanied by this very strong fall in tax revenues, while our balance of payments offered a strong external imbalance that turned the deleveraging of the Spanish economy into the great outstanding task, the great challenge that we have faced in this legislature. The first thing we had to do was show that Spain was able to turn the situation around and control public spending while we stopped the fall in income in absolute terms and as a percentage of gross domestic product. In this work we involved all the public administrations because we were playing the very viability of the State of self-government; I do not say since we had in luck the financing of the State of welfare. There were many voices that denounced in the years 2011 and 2012 that Spain could not bear the weight of three levels of public administration with their respective expenses. That was not the case, as is being demonstrated in the case of Spain in the correction of its public deficit. When that fiscal consolidation effort of all the administrations came to fruition and we began to meet our deficit targets, the countries of our environment and the financial markets began to regain confidence in Spain. In this way, we are dealing with the risk of rescue and, therefore, the danger of being subjected to harsh and surely inappropriate economic and social conditions in exchange for obtaining funding. What we did do is open up the financing of our economy, which would facilitate the current stage of economic recovery, of economic growth, behind that key of confidence. We have been able to retain our political sovereignty in order to get out of the crisis, since no one has imposed our policies and reforms on us, and we ourselves have chosen a policy of austerity, but a policy of balanced austerity, which means that we have not even been cut short, so that the deficit will end up getting out of hand, nor have we abused austerity any more from the account, which would have put social spending at risk and would surely have strangled the chances of growth and job creation. The results are there, in our figures. The deficit is controlled. Last year it closed at 5.69 % of the gross domestic product and this year it will reach at least 4,2 %, i.e. less than half of what was at the end of 2011. However, the social expenditure of the general government as a whole has remained at 27.5 per cent of the gross domestic product throughout the legislature, so that pensions have maintained their full volume, purchasing power, as have unemployment benefits and long-term unemployment benefits, and that health, education and social benefits policies have, of course, been sustained in a more than fundamental way, largely because the State has been indebted to finance autonomous communities and municipalities so that they can continue to carry out these welfare State-specific policies. Finally, we have ended the economic recession. Last year, in 2014, the economy grew by 1.4% on average annually, picking up the change that had taken place since mid-2013, and this same year, in 2015, we will grow by 3.3%. And if in 2014 more than 400,000 jobs were already created, in 2015 we will close with more than 600,000 new jobs, identify them in social security affiliates or in the labour force survey. These figures are the best result a country can achieve, they are not those figures that some denounce by calling them macroeconomic, they are the opportunity to develop its vital project on the part of our citizens. These figures are the result of policies that we have been practicing since the Spanish Government over the past three and a half years. You have to ask yourself, from now on, what do we have to do? The answer is: We have to follow the same path, the path that is making us overcome the economic crisis, the path that is making us recover the levels of well-being, the only path that is making us recover the job creation and, therefore, aspire that in a few years we can have the volumes of employment - for everyone, but especially for our young people - that we had at the beginning of the crisis. It's reasonable. I know that there are voices in this House that constantly rise up against what they call austerity policy. There they are. These figures of economic growth and job creation have exceeded all the forecasts of the international bodies of the European Commission and have, of course, amazed many of its members, who have repeatedly denied them from this rostrum, from the very beginning of the legislature. Or are they going to deny me that they have been amazed when they now come up to express their appreciation and opinions? Not to us, nor have we been amazed because we have understood them as the fruit of a policy, nor have we been surprised because they come from the efforts of Spanish society and we know what Spanish society is capable of doing; we have lived it. We experienced this when Spain was excluded from the euro foundation's career, when Spain in the 1990s had an unemployment rate of 25%, when Spain in the 1990s had a public deficit of 7%. We did not meet any Maastricht convergence criteria, and we were condemned - it was then said - to the second speed or to the third division, to the regional division of Europe. That is what the Spaniards have been able to overcome and have always done with the governments of the People's Party. Some of those who call for the end of austerity are the same ones whose lack of austerity caused those 3.5 million more unemployed, exactly the same ones. Others, who demonize austerity, share stifling economic recipes such as those we have heard this morning. They have to be classified as what they are, staggering economic recipes that end in financial corralitos and pension cuts. We have neither bank corralitos nor pension reductions. The Government proposes to follow a path of undiminished austerity, where the priority is policies that drive growth and job creation and where the only unrelenting expenditure is social spending, which qualifies our welfare State. The general government deficit target is set at at at least 2.8% of gross domestic product in 2016, with the following breakdown: the central government will have to close the year with a deficit of at least 2.2; the autonomous communities with at least 0.30 of GDP; social security will also have at least 0.30; and local corporations will maintain budgetary balance. In this way, Spain will be able to comply with the Stability Pact and will be able to abandon the excessive deficit procedure. The control of the public deficit in this legislature is the history of a collective success, it is the finding that all public administrations can work together for the common good and it is also a clear example of how the common good is also the sum of the benefits that each of the parties obtains. As I said to your ladies and gentlemen, the control of the deficit is one of the factors that contributed most to Spain's recovery of its international credit and to the fact that today we are financing ourselves at very low interest rates. In this scenario, all territorial administrations have achieved better financing, either because they have gone to the liquidity mechanisms offered by the Government or because their membership in Spain, together with the effort made, reduces their access to markets. The value of what we have achieved among all is precisely that, in which we have done it together. If that had not been the case, if the municipalities, the autonomous communities or the central administration itself had been left out of the effort, the credibility of Spain as a country, of its autonomous state, would have remained in question and doubted the viability of our model of territorial organization. I would like to insist that it has been a collective effort, first of all, because it defines us very well as a State and enlightens us on the path that we must all follow in order to achieve success in the face of the adventures of those who wish to prove fortune outside Spain, and, secondly, because we have been listening for three years to the fact that the State has called for a greater effort from the autonomous communities than from itself and that this is why some have not achieved their objectives. We have even been listening to the last few days, coming from the new autonomous authorities, those that were born after the elections on 24 May, that the state remains more demanding for the next few years with the autonomous communities than with its own accounts; that is not true and I want to take advantage of this appearance to explain it. The adjustment that has taken place since 2011 has been very balanced. The central government ended 2011 with a government deficit of at least 5,1 % of gross domestic product, the largest of all, and closed 2014 with a deficit of at least 3,50 %, after an adjustment of 1.60 points, minus 1.60 points of deficit reduction by the general government. The autonomous communities, for their part, have made a very similar adjustment of 1.68 points, compared to 1.60 - I insist - of the General Administration of the State, after the autonomous communities started from a deficit of at least 3.34 in 2011 and closed 2014 with a deficit of at least 1.66%. Thus, the figures allow us to continue to affirm that the one-time effort to reduce the public deficit has been made by the autonomous communities and less by the proposed objectives that we bring to the House. The adjustment of the local entities was achieved by 93 cents in these three years, but it has been of great importance because it improved its forecast of budgetary balance and transformed it into a surplus of more than 0.53% in 2014, offering scope for all administrations to meet the target committed to the path of reducing our public deficit. Finally, Social Security is the only one that has increased its deficit in this period, but I must admit that this deviation has been inescapable because we are talking about one of the basic pillars of the welfare state, the one that most protects citizens, the one that most reflects the commitment between generations, the one that best cohesives our society: pensions. Pensions are paid in all the territories, in all the autonomous communities, in all the municipalities of Spain, as is the case with unemployment benefits. I do not know whether you will agree with me, ladies and gentlemen, but I assure you that the Government and the People's Parliamentary Group are convinced that what cannot be intended is to reduce pensions. That was the red line marked by the President of the Government since the beginning of the legislature and which has been firmly maintained these years at the worst times. This is why the pensioners have retained purchasing power and why the expenditure on unemployment benefits has remained until unemployment has started to fall and the destruction of jobs has been halted. In the new stage that we have opened, whose objectives reach until 2018, the consolidation effort will continue, but it is not true, as has been said, that the autonomous communities will have to make a greater effort than the others. Between 2015 and 2018 the central government will have to make an adjustment of 3.3 points of GDP, until reaching a total reduction, since 2011, of 4.90 while the autonomous communities have, from 2014, an adjustment of less 1.66 points in four years, half as much as the state. Social security will be able to reduce its deficit by 0.96 points thanks to the improvement of the economic and labour situation. There are groups in this House whose concept of budgetary stability and financial sustainability is not far from the approaches of the Government and the People's Parliamentary Group, because in fact they voted in favour of the Organic Law of ratification of the Stability Treaty. What happens is that politics is like that, and then it has made those same groups systematically vote against the general state budgets that we have been bringing to this House. I now appeal to everyone, but specifically to those groups, given that fiscal consolidation is going to follow and that from now on it is the State that is going to make a greater effort to control the deficit, to support the proposal of these agreements. The second objective of stability that we must face - and we must do so with determination and urgency - is the reduction of public debt because it is a question of financial release of Spain from the rest of the world. The debt has increased markedly, especially at the beginning of the legislature, precisely to respond to the various needs that were irrefutable if we wanted to get out of the crisis. Indeed, our public debt, which stood at 743,531 million euros in 2011 - 69 % of our gross domestic product - has risen to 1,033,857 million euros in 2014 - 97,7% of GDP - and this expression is still heard in this House: How has it been possible? Basically, for three reasons, for three chapters. Firstly, because of the need for funding as a result of the public deficit itself, which continues to exist in Spain. That is why it must be reduced in order to reduce public debt and its presence, its weighting on gross domestic product. Secondly, because it was necessary to pay what was not paid in 2011, it was necessary to pay the invoices. This is the principle, which we have also heard this morning, recognized in this House even by members of the left, let us say, more ideological: invoices must be paid. We have not invented another formula, we have to pay them because, if not, behind that unpaid bills is the bankruptcy of the State, the closure of public services, the bankruptcy of countries. It is said that historically there have been cases, of what? Debt default? Of course there have been cases. Spain is one of the European countries that has suffered the most public debt defaults in history, in the 19th and early 20th centuries. What did that bring? That brought decadence, loss of well-being, that brought the impossibility of achieving economic development and brought a Spain much more delayed than the other solvent states. That's why he said that we had to pay everything we had to pay, we had to pay the commercial debts recognized already as public deficit of our administrations if they weren't paid. That meant paying for utilities, outstanding health bills, outstanding education bills, outstanding social benefits bills. Because those senior residences, those disabled care homes and those unpaid bills by non-governmental organizations that were effectively combating social marginalization and the risks of extreme poverty had to be paid. Other invoices had also to be paid - the obligations recognised as outstanding in budgetary terminology - which were in the major ministries for investment, such as the Ministry of Development and Environment in a special way. The whole legislature paying bills from the past. That is also positive because thanks to that, by paying those bills, we inject into the productive economy of our country, we save hundreds of thousands of jobs, tens of thousands of businesses, self-employed people and their businesses. We also did so with the large companies that are suppliers of these public services, largely multinational companies operating in our country. Therefore, the policy has been to inject this volume of public debt into the productive economy, paying invoices directly to companies. As a result, the budgetary restriction we have experienced in Spain has been compatible with an economic recovery from the second half of 2013, consecrating in 2014 with the closure of economic growth above 2% and an average of 1.4%. The other, all the less important, cause of the increase in public debt in our country has, of course, been the use of the MED for the consolidation of the banking sector, for the recapitalisation of the banking sector, which amounted to 3.8 points of debt in terms of gross domestic product. That's what a part of the left recognizes and identifies as the rescue of the banks. Can anyone seriously wonder what would have happened with Spain, with bank depositors, with savers and with small investors? Can anyone seriously think that the banking corralite, the financial corralite that we see today in a euro-member country like Greece, would not have been reproduced in Spain? Hasn't that been avoided by calling for MEDE funding to regain the credibility of our banking sector? How would bank credit have been recovered in Spain to small and medium-sized enterprises - as I had previously had the opportunity to report and so it appears today in certain information - and to increasingly accessible interest rates if we had not done this policy of bank consolidation? Is there any doubt as to the effectiveness of this policy when, as a country, we are already returning that debt that we are asking for from the MEDE? We are already returning it thanks to that economic growth and the revenue recovery that comes from that economic growth. Furthermore, let us not forget that at this time our contribution to the rescue loans of Greece, Ireland and Portugal also means that our own public debt has increased by 2.1 points of GDP in these three years. It is the process similar to what we are going to experience now and which was already announced by the President of the Government who will bring to the House to facilitate financial aid to Greece. This is the explanation of why debt has gone from 69% to 97% at this time, almost 98%, in three and a half years. It is an explanation that justified it at the time, but it does not mean in any way that we do not also have the urgent need to reduce those levels of public debt in our most immediate future. The markets, our creditors, have understood this so well that they have increased confidence in Spain as a result of the implementation of this budgetary policy, including the increase in public debt. That is what explains the fall in the risk premium and, of course, the lowering of all interest rates on public treasury issues. Why? Because they were valuing our ability to gain competitiveness, because they were valuing our ability to save and because they were valuing the deleveraging, the deleveraging of the private sector of the Spanish economy, which in these years has exceeded 35 billion euros in the deleveraging of non-financial companies and families, reducing very substantially in this period. They have also recognised and valued the control of the public deficit, which I insist is not that it has been compatible with economic growth, but that it has facilitated such growth and, consequently, job creation. Since last year they have also greatly valued our economic growth, the present but also and above all the future, as a factor added to debt sustainability in general. What makes a country's debt sustainable is its economic future, it is how that economic future presents itself, whether it is full of opportunities or, on the contrary, full of difficulties and shadows. In our case, in the case of Spain, the economic scenarios of growth for the following years until 2018 really guarantee a future of opportunities for Spain. That's why we're financing ourselves as we are, and that's why creditors believe in Spain. We are now in a process of stabilising public debt which in 2016 will begin to fall, of course also as a result of the decrease in the burden of the interest rates we pay and by the own effect of the autonomic stabilisers derived from economic growth. The forecast we bring you here is that the debt will increase from 98.9% of GDP from 2015 to 98.5%, which is a very modest reduction but which will continue to improve in the coming years to 93.2%, so that we will have fallen very significantly throughout the journey to 2018 by 5.7 points of our gross domestic product. Ladies and gentlemen, our prediction is that the evolution of the economy will result in an increase in tax revenue of 4%, even after the effects of the tax reform we are applying - which will be the next item on the agenda of this plenary - have been included - and that non-tax revenue will grow by 3%. As we have also announced, much of the consequences of this increased collection and the outstanding liquidation of 2014 will cause the territorial administrations in Spain, those that manage health, education and social benefits policies, to have an increase in significant resources for next year. I have already said that almost 70% of the budget of our autonomous communities and of our municipalities is devoted to social spending, and it is therefore normal, and we must celebrate, that they have the financial sufficiency to ensure the provision of such important tools for our well-being, for our society of equal opportunities. It is the same effort, I insist, that makes it possible for the Government also to reinforce the social expenditure that depends on the State's budgets, thanks also to the savings that the unemployment situation produces due to the improvement of the labour market. The provision for financing territorial administrations is in fact going to increase by almost 8 %. I insist that this will also help them to reduce their deficit, to meet the objectives that we bring to the House, while the revenues of the State after the divestiture are barely increased by 0.8%. Local corporations will also see significant increases in their resources for next year. Moreover, as I have already said on other occasions, the new economic situation allows for a new model of financing for our autonomous communities and our local entities, which is more just and which guarantees the provision of basic services to all citizens on an equal footing, regardless of where they reside, to be put in place as from next year. As I pointed out at the beginning of my speech, Spain will achieve its stability objectives by maintaining that policy that we call austerity, which we do not confuse with cuts in public spending. Our non-financial expenditure limit is set for 2016 at EUR 123,394 million, 4.4% less than this year, or what is the same, EUR 5,666 million less. The budget forecast is to maintain the available spending of ministries and increase only those items in areas that we consider to be of particular importance, of particular social significance. Of course, we are talking about pensions, we are also talking about resources in the fight against tax fraud, against labour fraud and also as a result of the implementation of the Lomce. It is possible to do this without making cuts because there are some items of expenditure that are decreasing due to the good positive developments that I have already noted, particularly in the labour market. We hope that the savings of the Public Employment Service will also be significant. Let us return - already to end - to the argument of the public deficit. If in these years we had not focused on the reduction of the deficit, we would not have improved the financing that Spain obtains and, therefore, now we would not be talking about that next year we will pay less interest. Nor would we have growth and jobs like the one we have now and, therefore, unemployment would not be reduced, with what we would follow, like those who try to swim against the waves, making an exhausting effort without being able to move from the site. On the other hand, I would like to stress that economic growth is the one that allows us to maintain spending without the need for cuts. Last year GDP grew by 1.4%, this year we expect it to do so by 3.3% and next year by 3%, all in real terms; in nominal terms we expect growth to be somewhat higher this year. Keep in mind that if the economy grows more than expenditure, this will mean a percentage that the expenditure will weigh downwards on that gross domestic product. In this way, expenditure which hardly varies in its total amount, last year represented 43,5 % of gross domestic product, this year will be 42 % and in 2016 will be 40,6 %. With expected income equivalent to 37.8% of GDP, our deficit next year will be 2.8%. Following the same path, government spending in 2018 will amount to 38,4 % of GDP compared to expected revenues of 38,1 %, which means that we will have reduced our public deficit to only 3 tenths of GDP, although to achieve this, it is precisely not to change economic policy and not to change budgetary policy. I'm done. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the government is going to work until the last day of this legislature. We owe it to this great country, to Spain, and we owe it to the Spanish citizens, who have managed to bring Spain forward with their efforts. It is time to return the efforts made. We cannot interrupt the good progress of the economy by proposing undesirable flexibilities in the public deficit or by delaying the approval of budgets for several months. If we did, we would be introducing elements of uncertainty that are never good, and less when we are precisely in an economic recovery, in a process of improvement, and what we have to do is to stimulate it to become more income but above all more employment opportunities. Therefore, the stability objectives for the period 2016-2018 and the state's non-financial spending limit for the next year that we are debating in Congress project the same philosophy of the government action of these four years in budgetary matters, a government action based on these premises of seriousness and rigour in the fulfillment of our forecasts and commitments and in stability, a government action that has obtained the recognition of the institutions, the recognition of international organizations, the recognition of our European partners and the recognition of the markets. That confidence is allowing us to move forward with increasing determination in economic growth and job creation. We are there because we have won it at once, we are there because of the efforts of the citizens and we are there because the Government has chosen the right policy against what has been done in other countries, as we are seeing these days, and against what we have often been asked to do - and I will not, of course, remember - on this half day. We are there and we want to continue to be there in the future in order to further improve and offer the Spanish new employment and welfare opportunities. That is why I call for the support of the House. Thank you very much, Mr President. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u54	Thank you, Mr. President. Minister, if you had any doubts about your intentions in presenting the State's General Budgets for next year, I assure you that your intervention has completely and absolutely dissipated me. You are in the campaign, you close this legislature as you started it, processing general state budgets thought not, as you claim, to serve Spain and the Spanish, to serve the general interest, but to serve their electoral strategy. You know what happened to their first budgets, which delayed them waiting for the Andalusian elections. I hope the same thing happens to these people. They are now anticipating a parliamentary procedure by taking advantage of the absolute majority that they still have, rather than anticipating the elections, as I have already told you on some occasion, because their policies, despite what you continue to say, have failed; and I do not say this to you, but have been told by the citizens in the last elections. They are more confident in changing their communication and using the budgetary procedure for political propaganda and not for changing policies, because they are doing exactly the same. They are policies that have resulted in more unemployment, devaluation of wages and expulsion of talent abroad, especially among our young people. They are policies that create precarious and poorly paid jobs. Today we have talked a lot about Greece. Well, look, together with Greece, we are the country of the European Union that has the most part-time workers for lack of another option. They have done nothing to boost a new pattern of growth and that is where the political debate lies. You keep almost the same more devalued, but you have no choice but to acknowledge in the documentation attached to the agreement that we are discussing today that employment productivity is negative for next year. Indeed, the forecasts of the government's macroeconomic scenario imply a cyclical recovery due mainly to external factors, favourable financial and monetary conditions due to the fall in oil prices, the proactive action of the European Central Bank and the depreciation of the euro. Thus, the primary balance forecasts are mainly based on these cyclical factors: recovery of public income and fall in expenditure on unemployment benefits and debt interest. The primary structural deficit barely varies in 2016 compared to this year, which reduces the credibility of the public debt reduction path, which is close to 100 per cent of gross domestic product. The government has set the non-financial expenditure limit for 2016 at EUR 123,394 million, which means a 4.4% drop compared to this year, keeping the spending available from ministries. The expected evolution of public spending is a cumulative reduction of 5 points of GDP by the end of 2018, according to the stability programme. Whatever they say, this forecast means that spending on education and health will continue to fall, which is reflected in growth well below nominal GDP and in maintaining the level of public revenue around 38% of gross domestic product. They shine for their absence some kind of additional measures to bring us closer to the eurozone average, which confirms that they give everything to the cyclical recovery of the economy, as I have told you, with the risks it carries for the stability and sustainability of the welfare model. In fact, his policies are undermining him. In 2016, the public deficit should be reduced by 1.4 percentage points of GDP to 2.8%, which amounts to some EUR 15 billion. To this figure is to be added approximately 5 billion of the probable deviation to be produced this year, bringing it to a total of almost 20 billion euros. The Government expects to adjust this figure for the effect of the economic cycle. Government revenues will increase by around 4%, as will nominal GDP growth, which translates into approximately EUR 15 billion. Expenditure on unemployment benefits will fall, which, in addition to the fall in unemployment, hides the reduction in the rate of unemployment and the interest on debt compared to this year, thanks, as I have said, to the policies of the European Central Bank. In any case, the Greek crisis has demonstrated the great external dependence and the great vulnerability that the Spanish economy still has, which has manifested itself in the rise of the risk premium during this episode. The recovery we have seen in growth, both in GDP and in employment, is due to that economic cycle and not to reforms that the Government has undertaken. That is why we continue to have weaknesses that manifest themselves in macroeconomic imbalances, such as the high public debt; the primary public deficit, the highest in the eurozone - only surpasses us Cyprus -; the continuing growth of public debt, which already accounts for 100 per cent of GDP, and the second highest unemployment rate in the eurozone. But they also manifest themselves in social risks, and they value them very little: the high long-term unemployment and the segmentation of the labour market that slows the increase in productivity, the growing increase in inequality and the non-sustainability of the welfare model. The most important issue is, therefore, how to raise the growth potential of our economy in the medium and long term, which necessarily results in increased productivity. We are seeing that, despite rising growth, employment continues to grow at higher rates and that wages remain stagnant. External factors, as well as short-term and temporary factors, are not sustainable in the medium term. In order for the adjustment not to end by affecting social spending again, it is necessary to carry out a serious and rigorous tax reform, which increases and stabilizes public revenues, and at the same time to revise the model of autonomous financing, since the autonomous communities devote 75 % of expenditure to basic public services, with the aim of bringing resources into line with the responsibilities for the provision of public goods and services and in line with the revenues they can receive. We must no longer punish them with a division of fiscal effort, despite what you have commented, which seriously discriminates against them, as has already been raised by several autonomous advisers at the last Fiscal and Financial Policy Council. He also spoke about the social security system. It is currently the Administration that is taking more risks in the short and medium term to meet the fiscal consolidation path. The Government is repeatedly using the pension shelter. The Reserve Fund has increased from 66,815 million 2011 to 39,520 on 1 July, 40% less than in 2011. The result is a model of precarious jobs and low wages, unintended part-time contracts that reduce workers' contribution income and ineffective measures, such as flat rates, which reduce income through contributions, and the relationship between contributors and pensioners. There is also a fall in interest rates, which translates into an increasingly small income for potential interest for the fund, as it has a part invested in Spanish public debt. In short, ladies and gentlemen, the budgetary policy that you propose to us and which outlines the agreement that you are proposing to us today, and for what you have commented, is to continue to cut the welfare state by 2016. The cut could be €5.2 billion less if a tax reform has not been approved that essentially benefits higher incomes. In particular, 1 % is saved by an average of EUR 1,706 for the remainder of the year, while 10 million taxpayers with less income are saved by an average of only EUR 5. Meanwhile, one third of children under the age of sixteen live in poverty. As long as child poverty is a scourge, lowering taxes on higher incomes and big corporations, ladies and gentlemen, is indecent, and whoever does it shows their values, not exactly very social. But it is also an economic clumsiness, because the redistribution of wealth, especially when targeting children, young people and families, has a very high effect on demand and productivity. Such redistribution is the most effective weapon against inequality and to boost our economy. As I said at the beginning, on this occasion they are reusing the budgets in electoral terms. The final reflection I want to tell you is whether you have really valued the social cost of your model. What limit are they going to put at that social cost? Ladies and gentlemen, pure propaganda. It is also true that, while improving the situation, the unemployment rate will not fall this year or the next 20%, or will be slightly below 20% next year, twice as much as the European Union and more than twice as much as the unemployment rate at the start of the crisis. According to the data, the general government as a whole is expected to close the year with a deficit of 4,2 % and the target of 2,8 % for 2016 is set, logically in line with the European stability programme submitted to the Commission. Well, while the deficit reduction for the central government is 0.7 points, from 2.9% to 2.2%, next year - a reduction of approximately 25% - the autonomous communities will increase from 0.7% to 0.3%, a decrease in principle to 50%. The reduction is therefore higher in the autonomous administrations which, as has also been said here by other speakers, must respond to policies as basic and essential as, for example, education and health. If we were really convinced that with the decisions we make in this House we do not influence the fate of our country, on the day-to-day lives of our citizens, what would really justify our presence in this House? It would be enough to be present in other institutions; it would be enough to be present in the Executive Council, in the Executive Committee of the European Central Bank because everything would be resolved there. No, ladies and gentlemen, it's not all settled. The differential facts that mark our economy and our own circumstances are not resolved, let alone. Even less so, it's all solved. One thing is to live in that global economy to which we are indeed a party and therefore depend, and another very different is to devalue the character of political action and of course the capacity to exercise from governments and to propose to this House proposals or reforms, economic reforms, public sector reforms, banking reforms, reforms of a country's labour relations, reforms of energy supply, reforms of trade distribution, reforms of the opening of our international trade, reforms that really change the physiognomy of a country and make it possible that in that country where there is a crisis, there are clear lights; lights, more than of hope, of well-being and of opportunities for all. That's whether we're doing politics or we understand that. Many times political action, of course, has critical answers from the citizenry, it would be missing more, if we weren't really, not demons, as was alluded to here, but angels, and here Jacob's ladder we don't know anyone, I don't know if you have circulated through it but we don't know anyone. Therefore, here are the representatives of this people, Spain, which has so many reasons, despite the crisis and despite the discouragement, so many reasons when we look at our history and our recent history, when we look at the consequences of our integration into Europe and the global world, there are so many reasons - I repeat - to say that it is worth it, because it is a dynamic society, with the capacity to undertake, with the capacity to create the instruments necessary to safeguard its social cohesion. That's what we have to understand, I understand politics that way. That is why, as I believe in the debate and believe in the contrast, let us talk about figures and let us talk about economic realities, although I understand - I have lived a lifetime in this, a long life already, and I am not referring to the government, but as an economist, as a scholar of the social sciences and in particular of the public finance - that we live different circumstances in this which some call evolution or cyclical exit from the economy. But allow me, ladies and gentlemen, to understand that many times they are difficult concepts to transfer to the great public opinion and much more when we live in a society that matifies everything - for example, one objective is the fulfillment of a guarismo with exact figures, but behind that there is a lot of politics, behind that there are a lot of decisions and behind that there is a lot of effort, and especially in the right or wrong of these guarismos there is nothing less than the well-being and progress of Spanish society - to return to those figures. The Spanish economy is growing, that can no longer be discussed, that was discussed last year, I had to contrast it obviously in the debates of budgets, spending ceilings and so on, but now we can no longer deny that we are growing; we can no longer deny that we are growing at the head of the euro area, we cannot deny that we are growing at the head of the developed world, as the International Monetary Fund said. It cannot be denied, this is already so obvious that what is heard are arguments that it is apparently what is happening throughout the developed world. Well, it doesn't happen; it doesn't. What is the European Commission's forecast of growth this year for Germany, France or Italy? And unfortunately, when they have to update the Greek one we will see where it is. But what happens to Spain? Isn't there something different about Spain? Why do we deny that something different is happening to Spain? What's that difference? Why do we deny reality if it's numbers? We are going to grow a 3.3 in average terms according to the government and coinciding with more and more agencies inside and outside Spain. In this period of growth that we are living, without inflation, in the first part of 2015, how much have we grown? A three and a beak already, almost four percent. How much is the job growing? It's hundreds of thousands of people, almost 1,500 affiliates every day. How much is that at annual rate? 3.4% or 3.5% of the annual membership rate. Has there been any time in Spain when all growth has moved, since we are talking about no inflation, to job creation? Never. We had other stages of much economic growth, with nominal higher gross domestic product growth, but this had never been the case. And here you go up to say that then the productivity of the work factor fails, interesting argument. Failing productivity! There's always something wrong! But wasn't it about creating a job? Wasn't that the big question? Was it not a question of opening up in Spain an intense economic growth, as we had never before had, in creating jobs? Why do we underestimate this job creation? Can we say with firmness, with certainty that the more jobs are created, the more inequalities exist in society? Does anyone seriously support this statement? Another denial of reality! We have never lived what we are living, ladies and gentlemen, never, nor did the oldest people in the place know about this situation. I am now speaking as if I were an analyst, a responsible politician or a member of the Spanish Government who has driven those policies to make that possible, for we have never experienced this. Let's look at the macroeconomic picture to see what it means. Have we experienced any stage where economic growth, which in 2015 is going to be 3.3%, comes entirely from domestic demand, i.e. consumption and investment? People are consuming, people are investing, that's the investment. And of course, more moderately, public administrations are also investing, with very modest final consumption. But all growth comes from national demand. However, our country's capacity to finance, that is, our country's position, is in surplus vis-à-vis the rest of the world. What does that mean? That the agents we make up Spain are saving enough to repay the world's external debt. That's what you mean. Had we lived this stage before? Did they know an economic projection of these characteristics? We had never experienced this situation in Spain. When domestic demand grew, the country really needed to ask the rest of the world for new resources and not now. What's behind all this? A really great advance in competitiveness. There is also a financial consolidation of the economic agents that are disbanding and also of the public administrations that is what we bring here as a government project, which is really the first step to draw up the General Budgets of the State for the year 2016. I have already said two unique features of the economy in our economic history. All the growth in employment and all the growth compatible with the financing capacity vis-à-vis the rest of the world, a sine qua non condition for continued confidence in Spain. It is a country that is doing what it has to do because if the creditors do not really turn their backs on us, as we are seeing in other countries also members of the euro, also members of the European Union. It is not enough to be a member of the European Union to generate stability and confidence. You have to present a picture like this, which is really unprecedented in the history of the Spanish economy. Ladies and gentlemen, this had never been the case before. Isn't this good? Isn't that positive? Why are we criticizing him? Why are we censoring him? To criticize the government? You don't have to. Criticism to the Government is made in another way: the positive of the situation is identified and alternative policies are offered. It says: I would be doing it that other way. By the way, what we are doing as a Government is the same as what we proposed when we were opposed. We said that the country had to be disbanded, because it was a mistake to make that economic and budgetary policy that were not lowering Spain's foreign debt. That's what we're changing. Ladies and gentlemen, it is true that the net external debt has not fallen, as I said in the speech and explained in figures. It has not come down, we still owe the rest of the world a figure very similar to that which we owed at the beginning of the legislature, but there has been a really big qualitative change. As I said in my speech, the qualitative change is that non-financial enterprises have been decommissioned in more than EUR 25 billion and families in more than EUR 100 billion. Look at the figures, ladies and gentlemen. They're really big numbers, which they call macro. That means that in each company there has been a decision to reduce their indebtedness and that has to do with the policies that we have been adopting here. It has to do with tax policies, such as limiting the deductibility of financial expenditure from corporate tax bases; it has to do with economic policy and tax policy in an intimate and profound way. That has happened and, as I said, families too are continuing to delude themselves. That is positive and that is what is allowing Spain no longer to be indebted to the rest of the world. We're doing this by the public administrations, and I've explained it before. I already said that I believe in the parliamentary debate, but when the arguments are heard and answered, not when we have the pre-written intervention to tell the government and the House. I've explained the process before. I have already explained that the debt of public administrations has been increased in such a successful process in terms of generating confidence that more than half of Spain's public debt is held by international creditors. They are not public creditors, they are not owned by countries or states, they are owned by investors and not only from Europe, but from all over the world, investment funds and pension funds from all over the world who have bought our debt. Why do they buy us the debt? Because they believe in Spain and they understand what we're doing. Even if we have to issue debt, we are reducing the public deficit and we are getting to grow; that is the equation that generates trust and credibility, and in that trust and credibility creditors find support. What is the difference compared to the rest of the world between Spain, which is a heavily indebted country, and another heavily indebted country such as Greece? One enters a very deep crisis and the other, as inspires so much trust, continuously generates a financial flow that allows us to grow and create jobs. That's what's happening to us as a country. Now, instead of putting this on the two scales, they go up to say that none of this happens and that this is a country that does not exist. ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u118	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the Government is bringing to the Congress of Deputies this afternoon for validation a royal decree-law of urgent measures to reduce the tax burden borne by taxpayers of the personal income tax and other economic measures. Tax policy has been one of the key instruments used by the Government in its fight against the economic crisis that has been affecting Spain for seven years. It has been an instrument that has allowed us in successive stages to recover, first and foremost, the level of public revenue. [[The lady]] It has allowed us to fight the excess of family debt and above all business debt, it has allowed us to sustain social spending, it has allowed us to stimulate entrepreneurship, it has allowed us to compensate for the sacrifices of citizens, it has allowed us to encourage consumption, it has allowed us to act with equity and social justice both in the sharing of the burdens of the crisis and in the return of efforts when we are beginning this recovery and it has also allowed us to deepen in the new scenario before us, a positive, hopeful scenario of economic growth and job creation. With fiscal policy it is very difficult to conceal the truth, firstly, because all citizens participate in it, we all pay taxes, and secondly, because the results are transparent and can be easily contrasted, so they can rarely be questioned in a truthful way. Ladies and gentlemen, months before the content of the tax reform that came into force last January was known, we have already heard in the debates in this House the slogan - it cannot be called otherwise - which tried to discredit it and since then we have not stopped hearing it. They are slogans with argumentative simplicity, born from the topics, which have not stopped repeating that fiscal policy, which came into force in 2015, tax reform, benefits the richest and harms middle and low incomes. As I cannot accept this approach, as I would like no doubt about it, I will devote a couple of strokes to explaining what policy has harmed people with fewer resources, as they say, and what policy this Government has done to the contrary. In the past in Spain a fiscal policy was made that allowed large companies to pay little taxes. In fact, in 2011 the effective rate on the positive accounting result of the large consolidated groups in Spain was 3,8 %, which made several of the large IBEX companies paying in Spain - because they are taxable in Spain - do so at zero effective rate in corporate tax. That was the case with a socialist government and with parliamentary support in this House. In the past, in Spain, a fiscal policy was made that led to the loss of €70 billion in collection in two years, money that should have gone to sustain the welfare state, but that instead triggered our public deficit and that of course, as we had occasion to denounce in this very House, is one of the causes of the loss of 3,400,000 jobs, jobs that were lost much more within the families of people with middle and low incomes. In the past, Spain had a fiscal policy in which the most modest savings taxed the same as the large returns of capital, however high they were. In the past, Spain did not want to combat the crisis by raising taxes or higher incomes or businesses with greater economic capacity. In Spain of the previous legislature the socialist government did nothing with the fiscal policy that served to fight the crisis and inequality and everything that it did only served to impoverish the citizens, who are the taxpayers. Instead, in these years our fiscal policy has fought the crisis and the loss of tax revenues, rising from the first day of the legislature the minimum rate of IRPF 0.75 and 7 points - almost ten times more - the maximum rate, which only affects the higher incomes. Our tax policy has raised taxes on large companies - here is the collection from large companies - so that they are the ones who have the most money to get out of the crisis, and they are contributing very effectively to getting out of it. In that July 2012 I had to defend the rise of VAT and did so at a crucial time for Spain. We did so by preserving the VAT of products of the super-reduced type to 4% and keeping in reduced rates practically the entire shopping basket, in addition to the essential services for the operation of the country such as tourists and transport services. So that tax increase, that share of the tax burden of 2012 was effective because we asked more from those who had more economic capacity - more effort to the higher incomes - and from the big companies, although we already said that we were going to propose those tax surcharges on a temporary basis. In addition, we have strongly promoted the fight against fraud, because there is nothing more harmful and unjust for fairness than not paying the taxes that belong to each one, and the result of that impulse we have managed to break the record of collection. Each year the Tax Agency has been collecting in the fight against fraud the equivalent of more than one percentage point of gross domestic product in Spain. In 2014 the largest collection in history was obtained, with 12,318 million euros, and the results of the first half of this year constitute the best start of an exercise: more than 6.5 billion collections, 20% more than in the first half of last year. In these three and a half years of the legislature, more than €40 billion has already been raised, around €5 billion more than in the previous four years. To this is added the collection, the accumulation of new tax information data on goods and rights abroad that have Spanish taxpayers. This new tax information amounts to more than €124 billion - of which no information was available at the beginning of the legislature -, which, I insist, implies a tax information medium as never before had been held in the Spanish Treasury, in the Tax Agency, on the value of goods and rights abroad. On the other hand, when we managed to implement economic recovery, we did a tax reform thinking above all of those sectors that had had to overcome the greatest difficulties due to the economic crisis - middle and low income sectors, families with children and people in charge of other persons with disabilities, whether ascendants or descendants -, thinking about helping companies create jobs, especially small and medium-sized and self-employed, and also thinking about stimulating economic growth to boost job creation. It has been a tax reform that distributes tax benefits equally equitably, so that average and low incomes are more beneficial, which, moreover, represent a very large majority of Spanish taxpayers. The numbers are very eloquent. With the new IRPF, 1,600,000 people stop paying this tax; even almost half, 750,000, who earn less than 12,000 euros a year, have stopped having payroll retentions since last January and have earned their full salary since the first day of the year. Definitively, the reduction in the IRPF is an obvious example of the progressive nature of tax reform. That is why, now that the economic situation allows us to lower taxes again, we have decided to advance the second part of the IRPF rebate, which was due to enter into force next January 2016, to the current July 2015. The new law of the IRPF is much more equitable, much fairer than that which we inherited from the previous legislature because it poses a lower tax requirement for middle and low incomes. In the previous legislature the minimum rate in the IRPF was 24%; our tax reform significantly lowers it. Since January it has been at 20%, and now, with the new intermediate rate, we have lowered it half more, and in January 2016 it will remain at 19%. Instead, we are not going to lower the maximum rate on what was already in the previous legislature. With the Socialist Party it was 45%; we took it for three years to 52%, realizing that it was the highest incomes that had to make the greatest contribution to facilitate economic recovery. From this month, from July, and with the entry into force of this decree-law, it will remain at 46 % and until January of next year it will not return to 45 %. The same applies to the savings rate. Compared to the 21 % rate set by the previous government in 2011 for all returns above EUR 6,000, the current government has introduced three tranches, with higher taxation for higher and lower returns for lower savings. At the end of the reform, the savings rate of up to €6,000 will be 19% and will reach 21% for returns between €6,000 and €50,000, and from that amount they will tax 23%, 5 points more than the tax reform of the Socialist Party's 2006 government, in which, in addition to the exemption of the first €1,500 dividends, all returns, however high, would tax 18%. Returning to the IRPF, if the average final reduction of the reform for all taxpayers will be 12,5 %, for a large majority it is in practice double. We must bear in mind that 72% of the declarants, no less than 14 million taxpayers, declare annual incomes below EUR 24,000. Where applicable, the average final reduction shall be 23,47 %. Moreover, declarants with incomes below EUR 18,000 - 12 million taxpayers, i.e. 58% of the total - will have an average discount of 31% -31.06% exactly -. Now, once the second phase of the reduction in the IRPF comes into effect, all middle-income and low-income taxpayers will pay less in their IRPF than at the beginning of the legislature. This is possible because during these years we have made it easier for us to get out of the economic crisis, because our economic reforms and our budgetary policy have led us to be at the head of the big countries of the European Union in growth and job creation and with better prospects than the rest of the countries of the European Union, even better than the countries that started a few years ago from an incomparably better situation than ours. In addition, we have improved the minimum family sizes that had been frozen for six years and have introduced aid to large families, single parents with two children and those who are in charge of persons with disabilities - that is the great novelty - of EUR 1,200 per year for each of the situations covered by the law. That is to say, we have not only supported the welfare state in these years despite the recession by maintaining the social spending of all public administrations at 27.5 % of GDP, but we have promoted fiscal equity, we have curbed inequality and we are promoting new aid to groups that need greater state support. Because really the policy of supporting these groups not only has to read it in the lines of public expenditure, which are essential, but in the tax incentives contained in rules such as the one we are submitting today to the House for consideration. For the self-employed, who also benefit from the IRPF rebate, we propose that the retention rate be reduced from this month to 15%. Initially the reform envisaged that the retention would remain at 19% this year and fall to 18% next year, but the economic situation today allows us to go further, makes it easier for us to lower those retentions that are similar to those already held by professionals who declare incomes below 15,000 euros. I believe that I agree with all your ladies and gentlemen that the role of the self-employed, their effort, their sacrifice has been fundamental during the crisis and is being even more key at this stage of economic recovery. That is why we now not only compensate you for the IRPF rebate, but we also lower your retentions so that you can better meet your professional needs and strengthen your business and activity. This is a measure affecting about 800,000 self-employed people, which will stimulate their economic activity and restore the type of retention to the levels that existed in 2011. In addition, the new self-employed, who are now starting their business, will have lower retentions during the first three years, 7% which will certainly help them to start up their businesses with a little less economic effort. On the other hand, among the tax measures with a social component that includes this royal decree-law, is the establishment of new exemptions for certain aids. Thus, public aid granted by autonomous communities or local authorities to meet basic needs up to a maximum of 1.5 times the public multi-effect income indicator is declared tax-free. In addition, the possibility of an embargo on benefits and aid granted by the various public administrations for basic needs is limited. Aid and assistance to victims of violent crimes and against sexual freedom and, in particular, to victims of gender-based violence are hereby declared exempt. Finally, aid granted to communities of neighbours for resettlement will also be exempted. Ladies and gentlemen, we can now tackle these tax cuts. We could not, much less at the beginning of the legislature, implement the fiscal policy we had hoped for; we were in economic recession. But the fiscal policy that we put into practice, as is now shown again in figures, was the right one for that situation. Today we can say that thanks to that fiscal policy we can now lower taxes and thanks to that fiscal policy and the effort that we have made - I insist, equitable and balanced - the economic situation in our country is changing and is doing so clearly for the better. There are many moments in the activity of a government, and in the decisions of a finance minister, in which it has to take measures that are not really what it would like but which require the country's situation, and the country's priority is certainly what matters. Therefore, if this is the case, as we are now seeing, the fruits are those that today allow us to go up to this rostrum and ask for the validation of a new tax reduction for all Spaniards and, especially, for those with less economic capacity. When we approved the tax reform last year, the Government's forecasts were not what we saw in the previous point, they were not so favourable or so positive. That is why we decided that some of the major reforms and tax cuts should be done in two phases, the first in January 2015 and the second in January 2016. We said that we preferred to be cautious, as we have been throughout the whole legislature in the forecasts and in the measures adopted. We remain cautious, but the evolution of the economy has been more positive than the Government thought at the time and, therefore, the improvement of forecasts supports the proposal we are making now. I remember when we made the budgets we said that the economy was going to grow by 2% and now, this very afternoon, we have brought, without further debate, that the economy will grow to 3.4%. We have not seen much discussion this afternoon about this, it was not interesting, no one has come to question that we are going to grow at this rate, with this intensity. When we made the budget we thought that job creation in full-time equivalent jobs would be 1.4% but in the first quarter of 2015, according to quarterly national accounts data, it is growing at an annual rate of 2.8%, i.e. twice as much as foreseen in the budgets. This is not sharing working time, they are full-time equivalent jobs. These two data show us the positive effect of economic reforms and tax reforms on the economy in just two months since they entered into force. In addition, economic improvement is increasing tax collection. The aggregated tax bases of the main taxes accelerated their growth rate in the first quarter of 2015 to 4.2%, well above the 2.6% that grew in 2014, after six consecutive years of decline. On the other hand, tax revenues in homogeneous terms have grown from January to May at an annual rate of 3.7%, which means that we are entering above what is foreseen in the General State Budgets for 2015. This increase in income is particularly significant in corporate tax, where split payments grew by 25,8 % until May, no doubt due to the growth of profits declared in the first quarter. In addition, VAT collection increased by 9.9% until May and by 10.6% for SMEs. Even the revenues by IRPF grew in homogeneous terms by 0.1% despite the decrease we made in January 2015. I would like to remind you that this decrease in the first few months of this year reaches EUR 1.4 billion, which we have used to lower the IRPF, while the collection has not only not fallen but has risen, very slightly, but has risen. Thus, the economic situation not only allows us to advance the reduction of the IRPF and the retentions to the self-employed, but it is actually inviting us to do so. The economy is improving more and more and that is why we must take advantage of it to continue deepening the tax reduction, convinced that in this way we are stimulating economic growth and employment at a time of the year also very conducive to stimulate economic activity, domestic demand and, through a greater wage gain resulting from the tax reduction, to promote consumption. Ladies and gentlemen, this afternoon's proposal, the proposal for a decree-law, involves injecting into the economy an additional EUR 1.5 billion to what we had already planned for this year. Thus, 20 million citizens and their families are going to notice this tax reduction from this very summer in their pockets as the retentions are going to go down again, as they did in January. In this way taxpayers will have more resources, more money for their spending decisions for what is left of the year. In order to implement this reduction, the Government has approved an interim fee that will apply to rents from January to December 2015 in place of the one expected at the beginning of the year. The same will happen with the savings rate as the discount planned for 2016 is also ahead of July 2015. Ladies and gentlemen, the royal decree I have the honour to defend before the House for its validation includes, in addition to this reduction in the income tax on natural persons, other measures, among which I would like to highlight the reduction in the bill of light due to the disappearance of the tariff deficit and the social measures for coal mining workers, both promoted by the Ministry of Industry, Energy and Tourism. Thanks to the various measures contained in the energy sector reform approved by this Government, the chronic tariff deficit in the electricity system has been eliminated and there has even been a surplus in 2015. This results in a reduction in the depreciation of capital and interest which is to be transferred to the consumer by an average reduction of 2,1 % of the amount of his invoice between August and December. On the other hand, this Royal Decree-Law includes a number of social measures to strengthen social protection for miners, making it easier to pre-retirement and making it exceptionally easier for companies that are also in the process of advancing their mine-exploitation programme before the planned date to have their allowances paid. The measures that have been agreed between the Ministry of Industry and the most representative trade unions respond to a fall in the national production of coal, greater than expected, which foresees that from 2018 only workers who actually meet the working conditions in competitive companies and certainly also more clearly in what is known in the activity as open skies will be kept in the sector. In conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, the final months of the legislature are showing us the results of the government's policies in these years. Tax measures, structural reforms and budgetary policy present us with a country that is growing steadily, whose economy is at the forefront of the growth of the most advanced countries, creating jobs at a higher rate than the average in the European Union and which therefore maintains growing and flourishing tax collection rates. The first half of 2015 is showing us that the good data from 2014 was the foreground of a recovery that has greater vigor than expected then - I speak of 2014, of what we all saw since 2014-, and invites us to continue moving along the path that will definitely lead us to overcoming the crisis. To put an end to the high unemployment rates that have arisen throughout this phase of crisis has been the most important objective of government policy; it has been the objective to which all the efforts made in these years have been directed and which are now beginning to bear fruit in the form of new employment, entrepreneurship and tax cuts. The reduction of the IRPF has shown, in this first half of the year, that it has been a valuable and effective tool to compensate people with low and middle incomes, especially for the efforts made these years, and to stimulate their economic activity, consumption and with it, the economy and employment. That is why the Government proposes to move forward with the second phase of the reduction of this tax and in doing so consolidates a policy of lower fiscal demands at this stage of economic recovery, which will have to have continuity in the coming months in order to benefit the citizens and the entire economic activity of our country. This is the purpose that encourages me to request on behalf of the Government the validation of this royal decree-law to the House. Thank you, Madam President. Thank you, ladies and gentlemen. ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u134	Thank you, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, our parliamentary group, as has already happened in the previous debate, is part of the general approach of what the role of direct taxes is. They have two functions: the first is to generate or contribute to the well-being of citizens, raising the income needed to meet the needs of public goods and services, i.e. those in which use is not directly associated with respect to payment. But they have a second role no less important, which is to contribute to social justice through a process of redistribution of wealth, which cannot be attributed to indirect taxes. Since both functions are equally necessary, it is necessary to analyse this royal decree in the obviously tax reduction part of the IRPF, considering the two dimensions. I am already telling you that we are going to support this royal decree for a fundamental reason, for the reason that if the lower incomes and the average incomes are actually the ones that have basically borne the increases of the IRPF, if the economy goes well, it is logical that they are relieved from the fiscal pressure even if it is a limited and temporary relief.
