DEBATE CONCERNING THE AUTHORIZATION OF THE NATIONAL DEFENCE UNDER ARTICLE 17.1 OF ORGANICAL LAW 5/2005 OF 17 NOVEMBER 2005: - APPLICATION FOR AUTHORISATION BY THE CONGRESS OF THE DEPUTED FOR THE DEPLOYMENT AND PARTICIPATION OF THE ARMED FORCES IN THE MILITARY OPERATION OF THE EUROPEAN UNION IN THE MEDITERRANEAN CENTRAL MERIDIONAL, EUNAVFOR MED. [[File number 095/000008]]
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u150	We continue with the debate on the request for authorization by the Chamber provided for in article 17 of the Organic Law 5/2005 of 17 November of the National Defence, of the agreement of the Council of Ministers of 10 July 2015 requesting authorization from the Congress of Deputies for the deployment and participation of the Armed Forces in the European Union military operation in the southern central Mediterranean. The Minister of Defence has the floor for his presentation.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u151	Thank you, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, I appear before you to request the authorization provided for in Article 17 of Law 5/2005 of the National Defence for the participation of Spanish troops in missions abroad. First I would like to record the Government's condolences for the death of a civilian guard officer from the Tedax Service in Almeria, Don Emiliano Quintana Fernández, who has lost his life in an accident this afternoon at fifteen o'clock. Returning to the matter, on this occasion I ask for your vote in favour of our troops joining the operation of the European Union Eunavfor Med, which is called. This participation will take place under the terms, conditions and circumstances set out in the Council of Ministers agreement of 10 July 2015, which has been referred to this House by the Government and which I shall then explain in its context, possible developments and more specific details. As you will recall, the conclusions of the Council of the European Union of December 2013 began with the phrase: Defence matters. The conflicts that today affect areas that are increasingly close to our fellow citizens, our territory and our interests, as well as those of our closest allies, had not yet exploded with all their intensity. Defence matters because security is a need felt and required by society, whatever its political, economic and social situations and, in particular, in those that have developed states of law, democracy, freedom and justice that by their very condition make us more easily attackable by those who do not respect any of these values so hard to achieve throughout our shared history. This is the eighth time that I have appeared before this House, either in plenary or in the Commission, to ask them to vote in favour of the participation of Spanish soldiers in missions abroad, all of them with the common objective of providing security, that is, stability that will enable them to develop progress and well-being to those who do not have them. Well, of those seven previous requests, at least six are trying to face circumstances directly related to the root causes of the mission that brings us here today. I speak to you, ladies and gentlemen, of our operations in Mali, where we train the new armed forces of that country, an essential task also for our own security. I am also speaking to you about our air support from Senegal to the French contingent Barkhane, which is carrying out the fundamental task of containing fundamentalist terrorism in the Sahel. Our work in Central Africa, where we help to consolidate an irrevocable instrument for the rule of law and prevent it from becoming another failed State, has been to contribute to the failure of a systematic genocide between two factions within the country. Ladies and gentlemen, I am also speaking to you about our efforts to train an Iraqi army that can protect its citizens from the barbarism of Daesh and develop the consolidation of the state in that country, Somalia and Afghanistan, Turkey and Lebanon. Finally, I am talking to you about the responsibility that Spain has assumed for global stability and I am grateful for the majority of the support that these operations have had in this House. All these actions - probably given some imperfections in the functioning of the international community and also some omissions - have a direct link with what I am bringing here today. Today I come to present to you a crisis management operation in the Mediterranean, a small but totally essential part of the overall response that the European Union has decided to give to the dramatic humanitarian situation in the Central Mediterranean, particularly linked to the chaos that Libya has been experiencing lately. As you know, this is a particularly complex crisis of which mass immigration by sea is only a symptom of major evils, a mere effect of the political, economic and social circumstances that produce it and that we have to help resolve among all. To this major underlying problem - which is not addressed by the measures we are going to take in this operation which I am presenting to you if your ladies and gentlemen are willing to approve it - we will have to confront it if we do not want the problem to become endemic. However, as I say, what we bring here today is urgent to remedy real-time tragedies while I am addressing your ladies and gentlemen with this request. We must help to alleviate the tragedy immediately, but, I repeat, it should not be forgotten that, in addition to the above-mentioned insecurity and poverty of all kinds, other root causes that generate this situation lie in factors of political instability which have their most tragic consequence in armed conflicts and which destroy the African continent and the Middle East, increasingly close, by the way. We must be aware that the drama we see in the Mediterranean, in this case in the Central Mediterranean, also has a profound and dramatic reflection on the vast and multiple transit routes throughout Central and North Africa. We must not forget that many of the people who suffer this tragedy, no matter how dramatic the odyssey they may or will live in the sea, never reach the Libyan coasts. From the Ministry of Defence, we follow with special attention not only the situation at sea, but also all the possible effects that this situation could have on a large area of North Africa. The fragile situation in Libya and the current permeability of Libya's borders mean that any action can have a direct impact on neighbouring countries. As I have stated in previous appearances, this region represents a challenge for Spanish foreign action, as it brings together various challenges to which our country, together with other allies of the European Union and the international community, must respond with active policies. As I say to you, the situation has been aggravated by the unsustainable situation in Libya. The origins of the Libyan conflict have long been debated profusely in this House, which, as some of you will recall, approved with an overwhelming majority the participation of Spain in the NATO operation of 2011. We have also dealt extensively and more precisely with the implicit and explicit risks to our country and to the whole of the European Union that instability in the southern Mediterranean, particularly in the Gulf of Sirte and throughout the Libyan coast. Unfortunately, since July 2014, the situation has gradually worsened. The country is facing a deep political and security crisis, the complexity of which is accentuated by the growing presence of Daesh-affiliated groups. The United Nations is leading a political dialogue process to push for a ceasefire agreement and the creation of a Government of National Unity in Libya, whose legal framework would be provided by a new constitution. However, and despite some good news we have received these days, the continuing incidents and disagreements between the factions involved continue to plunge the country into instability that makes it impossible for us to have a valid dialogue. As a result of this situation, the Central Mediterranean route has become the most used route for the illegal traffic of migrants to Europe. This occurs mainly from the Libyan coasts to Malta and to the Italian islands of Lampedusa and Sicily. Although several departure points were previously used on the entire Libyan coast, and even in its vicinity, the main boarding sites are currently located on the western coast of Libya, controlled by the Tripoli-based National General Congress. Before starting the journey from there, immigrants have crossed the Sahel using several land routes that originate in the Horn of Africa or in West Africa and converge in southwest Libya, in Sabha to be more concrete. Many of them must remain a good season in Libyan territory, trying to obtain by any means the money necessary to embark on Europe. Over the past two decades, more than 20,000 immigrants died using the Mediterranean routes, most of them in accidents that occurred precisely on that central route; only in 2014 the migrants drowned in those waters of which it is known reached the figure of 3,500. In the same year, 170,000 people had to be rescued by the various aircraft services involved. As far as 2015 goes, the dead are almost 1,500, although the efficiency in the rescue of operations Triton and Poseidon continues, in which the Civil Guard participates within our jurisdiction. These figures clearly express the magnitude of the tragedy we are talking about. As the President of the Government pointed out this morning, bringing the feeling of the European Union, we could not remain unfettered in the face of an unacceptable humanitarian situation, which also has important implications of all kinds for those of us who are part of that political unity. We have probably been thinking too long, talking about why and how to deal with the situation, how to implement the multiple crisis management instruments available to us, without any definitive action being taken to resolve this issue. In order to overcome this impasse and as a responsible European partner, we have, from the very beginning, promoted the process of planning, approving and launching the Eunavfor Med operation that I am presenting to you today. Its aim is to combat the ways and forms of action of the mafias who traffic with human beings, and whose immediate priority is to prevent more people from dying at sea. It is painful to see how sometimes even international organisations with relatively agile mechanisms of political action, such as the European Union itself, consume a significant amount of time - vital in many cases such as this, of course - until they are able to react. Perhaps the lack of a shared strategy in external action and defence and security is one of the causes. I hope that the decisions taken by the European Council to which the President of the Government referred this morning will serve this purpose. Not to look, and as an immediate consequence not to address security and defence problems, is to aggravate them. That is why I am glad that the High Representative has decided, and the political leaders have agreed, to launch this security strategy. That is why the process was reactivated on 19 April, immediately after the tragic sinking 70 miles north of the Libyan coasts of a ship in which 700 immigrants were travelling. Thus, four days after that catastrophe, the Heads of State and Government of the European Union, meeting on an extraordinary basis in Brussels, decided that the Union would mobilize all the means at its disposal to prevent the continued loss of life at sea and to address the root causes of this human tragedy, in cooperation with the countries of origin and transit. Therefore, and beyond the activity already carried out by Frontex in coordination with several Member States, and that of the Italian Government in the exercise of its powers, the European Union decided to use all the means which, supported by the relevant legislation, would be necessary to tackle the tragedy. At the extraordinary Council held on 23 April 2015, in particular, urgent measures were taken in four priority areas: strengthening the European presence at sea, strengthening common responsibility and internal solidarity, preventing illegal migratory flows and combating, above all, networks of trafficking in immigrants, in accordance with international law and mainly through systematic efforts to detect, capture and destroy vessels before they can be used by criminals who traffic in persons. To this end, the Council requested the High Representative of the Union for Foreign Affairs and Security Policy to begin preparations for a possible operation within the framework of the common security and defence policy. High Representative Mogherini then briefed the United Nations Security Council on the possible military operation, stressing that any action by the European Union would, as it could not otherwise be, be in accordance with international law. At the same time, it requested the Security Council to adopt measures or resolutions to support the actions of the European Union. On 18 May 2015, the European Council, by Decision 2015/778, approved the concept of crisis management for the operation in the Southern Central Mediterranean and the decision for its establishment. Similarly, the Italian vice-admiral Enrico Credino was appointed commander of the Eunavfor Med, and his operational headquarters was established in Rome. As we have seen, the fundamental mission of the military operation is to disrupt the business model of illicit trafficking networks and trafficking in persons through systematic procedures to identify, capture and dispose of vessels and means used or available to traffickers. The Decision also provides that Eunavfor Med will cooperate with the Member States and establish appropriate coordination mechanisms with other relevant and linked agencies and bodies, in particular Frontex, Europol and Eurojust, the European Asylum Support Office and the Common Security and Defence Policy missions. In the same act, the processes for the provision of the mandatory Initial Military Directive and the corresponding Operations Plan were initiated. Both instruments have been designed in accordance with the requirements of the main existing legal instruments and, in particular, the following provisions: the 1982 United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea, the 2000 Protocols against the Illicit Traffic in Migrants by Land, Sea and Air and to Prevent, Suppress and Punish Trafficking in Persons, Especially Women and Children. Both texts complement the United Nations Convention against Transnational Organized Crime. The 1974 International Convention for the Safety of Life at Sea, the 1979 International Convention on Search and Rescue and the 1976 Convention for the Protection of the Marine Environment and the Coastal Region of the Mediterranean have also been included. Finally, the 1951 Geneva Convention relating to the Status of Refugees and international human rights law. The fundamental link between these conventions and, therefore, the humanitarian element on which the whole operation is built is undoubtedly the duty to assist people at risk at sea and to bring survivors to a safe place. The ships assigned to Eunavfor Med will therefore be ready and equipped to carry out these tasks under the coordination of the competent rescue coordination centre. With the legal framework clear, work began, as I told them, to close the operation. In this way, the Post Generation Conference for headquarters was held in Brussels on 28 May. That same day we passed on to our partners that we would contribute, obtained in their case the approval of this House which I come today to ask for, with at least eight of the headquarters posts. At the same time, it was reported that it was intended to participate in the Force Generation Conference conditional on the timing of the launch of the operation and, of course, on the parliamentary authorization I have just mentioned. The following days were devoted to negotiations to approve the initial military directive, the process of which was not without difficulties. As you will suspect, the problems were mainly due to the discrepancies between some of our partners on the determination of the ports of landing of the immigrants, once they had been rescued by the ships of Eunavfor MED. In fact, the Military Directive was adopted on 3 June because it was agreed to postpone the debate on this controversial issue. In any case, it was stated that the matter would have to be resolved, as is natural, before the approval of the Operations Plan. At the meeting of the Political and Security Committee on 9 June, this political guide was provided under two fundamental premises: the principle of non-refoulement of persons rescued at sea was reaffirmed and it was established that the procedure for their landing would be the same as applied in Operation Triton de Frontex. Landings will therefore be carried out at ports in Italy or, in the event of an emergency, in Malta, always by coordination with the European Border Agency. Once this delicate and technically very complex issue was resolved, it seemed that the approval of the Operations Plan would be peaceful, but the process was equally laborious. Among the Member States, the European Union's General Staff and the legal services of the European Union's external action service, a total of two hundred and fifty comments were sent to the first draft, which contained a few leaflets, allowing an idea of the complexity of the document. Many of those comments dealt with the three fundamental aspects: the need or not to have a United Nations Security Council resolution covering all or part of the operation, the requirements and the need for the consent of the Libyan authorities with all its implications, and, finally, the definition of the legal requirements for commencing each phase of the operation. Finally, we managed to move forward with the Operations Plan that was approved on 19 June. Three days later, on 22 June, the Foreign Affairs Council agreed to start the operation in its Decision 2015/972. The initial Operations Plan envisages three distinct phases. The first, which for the time being is the only one that can be launched, is carried out in international waters, in accordance with the provisions of the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea. In addition to maintaining and strengthening shipwreck rescue efforts, this first phase is aimed at supporting, detecting and monitoring immigration networks through the collection of information on high seas and air patrols. In this regard, and in the light of possible doubts that might give rise to legal protection, let me clarify to you that, on the high seas, in accordance with the above-mentioned convention and applicable national and international law, States may intercept, if authorized by the flag State, vessels suspected of illicit trafficking in immigrants in order to take appropriate measures in respect of such vessels, persons and cargo - in quotation marks - carried by them. [[Rumors]] In turn, the plan envisages a second phase whose adoption and implementation has not yet been decided and which would also require a resolution of the United Nations Security Council. This second phase would be divided into two stages. The first would be aimed at addressing, registering, seizing and diverting vessels on the high seas suspected of being used for illicit trafficking in persons under the conditions provided for in international law. The second, which would require the consent of the coastal State concerned, would be to address, record, seize and divert suspicious vessels on the high seas, territorial seas or inland waters of that State, in accordance with the conditions of the United Nations Council resolution and/or the request or consent of the State concerned. [[The Lord]] Finally, the plan proposes a third phase that would also require a United Nations Security Council resolution or the consent of the coastal State. This phase would consist in taking all necessary measures against any suspicious vessel under the conditions laid down in that resolution or with the consent of the coastal State on its territory. [[Rumors]] For the time being, Member States are only planning to undertake the first phase of the operation, namely the deployment of ships and aircraft to detect migratory traffic in international waters north of Libya and south of Italy and Malta. As I have already said, the transition between stages will be subject not only to their legal coverage, but also to the political control of the Member States. Spain will continue to maintain a position of full cooperation with the mitigation and termination of this unfortunate situation. The Council of the European Union will be responsible for assessing whether the conditions for going beyond the first phase have been met..., [[Rumors]]

ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u166	Thank you very much, Mr. Sanz. Mr Lleonart has the floor for the Group of the Progressive Union and Democracy.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u167	Thank you very much, Mr President. Minister, go ahead and our parliamentary group, UPyD, will vote in favour of the request for authorization by the Congress of Deputies for the deployment and participation of the Armed Forces in the European Union military operation in the southern central Mediterranean. But let us put into context what we are discussing here for those outside this Chamber who are seeing this debate on television or on the Internet and for those who, despite the minister's lengthy intervention, have not been clear on the subject. This is a European Union military response initiative within the framework of the common foreign and security policy to control illegal migratory flows across the Mediterranean, especially, but not exclusively, from the failed State of Libya, which includes the possibility of preventive attacks against the bases of organized crime groups operating on the Libyan coast or in other countries. It has the legal support of the UN and derives from an initiative of the European Mediterranean countries, including Spain. It also faces the difficulty of militarizing the response to illegal immigration, and is likely to contradict some of Frontex's initiatives in the Mediterranean. But there is the positive experience that this type of military initiative had against the piracy groups operating off the coast of Somalia that have been largely dismantled. The situation in the Mediterranean has become unsustainable. Over the past 15 years, it has become a cemetery for more than 22,000 migrants and refugees looking for a new opportunity on the European continent. In 2014, 3,400 people died trying to reach the European coast, many of them fleeing humanitarian crises caused by wars and conflicts such as those in Syria, South Sudan, Somalia, Eritrea, Yemen or Libya itself. As far as the year goes, the estimated number of people who have lost their lives at sea exceeds 1,900; a figure that could increase over the next few months, as, encouraged by the good weather, a greater number of people go to sea to try to reach Europe, putting their lives in grave danger. There is therefore a growing need for a substantial and urgent increase in maritime search, rescue and rescue operations, as well as a substantial change in European immigration, refugee and asylum policies. People need safe and legal ways to escape from conflict zones; there are very few safe and legal channels that they can choose to get to the protection and safety of the European coasts. With Europe's land borders sealed by very restrictive migration policies, many people end up being forced to fall overcrowded into the hands of unscrupulous smugglers; even before reaching that point, many have made long and risky land trips under conditions that threaten their lives. The restrictive migration policies of Europe that we have mentioned before mean that, by omission, we are putting some of these most vulnerable people, the most vulnerable people in the world, in even greater danger, causing them more suffering if they can. Europe and transit countries have a responsibility to ensure that their policies guarantee the right to seek asylum and respect human dignity. In this regard, since UPyD we believe that as many measures as are necessary for the restoration of operations specifically aimed at preserving people's lives through the active search, rescue and rescue of human beings at sea, in line with Operation Mare Nostrum, of proven effectiveness. Bet within the European Commission on the opening of new channels to facilitate legal and therefore safe access for immigrants and asylum seekers on the territory of the European Union. To substantially increase the current quota for resettlement of refugees in Spain, in line with the proposals and criteria of the European Union, in line with the Acnur recommendations, supported by the International Organization for Migration and by different humanitarian entities, and to participate in the proposals for relocation of persons in need of international protection, who have already arrived in numerous groups in some countries of the Union, according to the mechanisms that the EU itself has established to activate in crisis situations. To this end, it is also important to ensure adequate means and resources to ensure the correct reception and integration of these refugees, and particularly for those most vulnerable, including the request for funds from the European Union, which makes available a budget line for these quotas. To promote within the European Union a truly harmonised policy on immigration, asylum and shelter, as well as a management of migration flows that focuses on addressing the challenges in countries of origin and transit of refugees and immigrants seeking to reach Europe, not only from the perspective of border security, but also from the perspective of the drive for human, social, institutional and economic development, the promotion of democracy and fundamental rights and the generation of opportunities in those countries through coherent policies that effectively facilitate development cooperation. Thus, in collecting and finalizing, we believe that the proposal we are debating today does not offer guarantees for the Mediterranean case given the coincidence of armed conflicts in the region. We also believe that there are a number of measures that we have just outlined that would address immigration from a global perspective and that the Spanish Government could commit itself to promoting and implementing. However, this operation at least demonstrates the political will of the European Union and the world's major military powers to solve the problem of illegal immigration into the Mediterranean, not only civil but also military, and we therefore reiterate that our group will vote in favour. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u168	Thank you very much, Mr Lleonart. Mr Agirretxea has the floor for the Vasco PNV Group.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u169	Thank you, Mr. President. I am not saying that what is going to be done does not have to be done, but we all agree that this is not the only thing that needs to be done. The consequences are limited, but you do not go to the bottom of the issue once again, and you can say: And to me what you tell me!, I am the Minister of Defense. In the background, it seems that what is intended here is that at least the gateway to Europe is controlled. We can read articles and reports that have been done about what people are suffering on these ships, how they are boarded, the money they pay, how they are tricked and then left drifting by dialing with a phone that detects the GPS of the nearest coastguard. Those who have rented the boat escape, after making them pay more than 5,000 euros to each crew member, leaving them drifting. This situation obviously needs to be resolved, but we are talking about a human drama and with these people we must do something. Therefore, looking above is not solving the problem. We must be more ambitious, bolder and more comprehensive; and when I say it in its entirety, I say it in the sense of analyzing and solving things in an integral and not in part. It is a truly dramatic problem and I believe that the European Union has an important responsibility. This is one more step and I hope that the Government will propose solutions to tackle this human tragedy, as it is first about saving lives, protecting refugees and seeking political solutions in the places that need it. Thank you very much, Mr President.

ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u172	Thank you very much, Mr. Tardà. Mrs. Pérez Fernández.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u173	Thank you, President. Ladies and gentlemen, Mr Minister, Operation Eunavfor Med presents it textually as a solution to prevent the continuing deaths at sea and to address the root causes of the human tragedy we face. We are convinced that military solutions will not serve to address the root causes of the human tragedy we face, that - do not forget - in the case of Syrian, Afghan or Somali refugees are wars in their respective countries, or, in the case of Eritreans, the very harsh dictatorship from which they flee. Military intervention will only manage to divert this flow of people fleeing the war to other points of the coast and the use of other more precarious means to continue trying to reach Europe, turning their journey into much more dangerous, if any, than it is now. Ladies and gentlemen, the European Border Control Agency itself, Frontex, recognises that the vast majority of immigrants - 80% - who risk their lives trying to reach Europe are not economic immigrants, but refugees or possible refugees who should receive international protection, and to whom Europe should legally recognize that right in application of the international conventions signed by the Member States on protection and asylum, including the Spanish State. While this is the case, the Spanish Government's action at the June European Council, as well as the meeting in Luxembourg on 9 July last, has been embarrassing and completely lacking in commitment. At the moment, there are two countries throughout Europe that have prevented the closure of a distribution of potential refugees arriving in Italy and Greece; one is Spain, demonstrating that spirit that already appears in the law gag or in the reform of the Law of Universal Justice. The Secretary-General himself of the United Nations expressed his concern about this European Union operation in order, in principle, to combat the trafficking of immigrants in the Mediterranean; and not only Mr. Ban Ki-moon, but many associations that work and know this area well and believe that the priority is to save lives and offer humanitarian assistance. In that connection, you will understand that, at least, this measure, this proposal, is controversial to us and that the Spanish State's unconditional support for this action is contradictory, as long as it continues to prevent the agreement on the distribution of refugees from being closed. The BNG understands that repressive measures and the militarised response of the EU will not stop the waves of migration that have very deep causes. In fact, every time repressive measures were taken and an attempt was made to block a migratory route, another one was immediately opened; neither the ever-higher fences or walls, nor the indignant concertinas managed to reduce the flow of immigrants. What has been achieved is that these flows enter Europe through increasingly dangerous and risky routes. Priors, such as Operation Mare Nostrum, launched by Italy, did not prevent more than 150,000 immigrants from being rescued in that operation. And with the European operation Triton, which replaced the failure of Operation Mare Nostrum, since the end of 2014 we have already seen that the number of deaths in the Mediterranean has not been reduced. For us, this new operation is also not intended to solve, or even to alleviate, the situation of thousands of people who, in desperation, launch themselves across the sea, but its fundamental objective is to prevent those people from even leaving the coasts of Africa, so that they do not even have to be rescued. The economic resources that this operation is going to take, in our opinion, of course, would be much more justified - I conclude, Mr President - if they were intended to save lives and to provide humanitarian protection and asylum to the thousands of people who arrive on the European coast. For these reasons, ladies and gentlemen, we cannot support the proposal before us. Thank you, President.

ParlaMint-ES_2015-07-15-CD150715.u175	Thank you, Mr. President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, my first words are to add to our sympathy for the death of the civil guard in Almería. At this very moment in which I address all of you, and in complying with the agreements of this House, our country has displaced troops from the Army and the Navy to Afghanistan, Lebanon, Iraq or the Horn of Africa, to cite a few examples. The men and women of our Armed Forces take this effort and risk their lives because the Congress of Deputies - we, ladies and gentlemen - estimated that this served the interests of Spain, fulfilled our international commitments and contributed to peace and stability in the world. Ladies and gentlemen, today the government is asking Parliament to allow our military to participate actively in the operation designed by the European Union to combat the migratory mafia in the Mediterranean. It does so for the same reasons that justified sending our forces to overseas on previous occasions. I'll try to prove it. Ladies and gentlemen, we served Spain's legitimate interests in Afghanistan, Lebanon or Iraq and were situations thousands and thousands of miles from our territory. Well, if in these cases the distance was not an excuse for the House to understand that national interests were at stake and that, therefore, our Armed Forces should participate in the operation, in conflicts that posed a distant but direct threat, either to the security of Spain, to world stability or simply for humanitarian reasons, it seems more logical and reasonable that we should act decisively when the scene of the problem, of the tragedy, is the Mediterranean Sea, which does not need to be remembered to bathe our shores, when one of the focus of that problem is just 14 kilometres from Tarifa or Punta Carnero, when we are barely separated from Ceuta or Melilla. I was telling you, ladies and gentlemen, that the second of the reasons that justified sending out expeditionary bodies was to make our international commitments good and in any case to be loyal to our allies. Well, this is an operation of the European Union and it is a European operation because it is a European problem that we are facing. It is true that Spain is directly affected by the issue because our country is a destination of illegal immigration through the Mediterranean and has been for some time in a preferential way, but illegal immigration through the Mediterranean is not a question of Spain, Italy, Malta or Greece, just as eastern immigration is not a problem of Poland, Hungary, Bulgaria or Romania. This is a clearly European problem, which affects us all in the same way, for which we are all responsible and for which the Union must give a solution, a clearly European response. In 2014, we received 600,000 legal immigrants. In the first six months of this year and only through the Mediterranean it is estimated that they have reached our shores about 150,000, 83% more than in the same period last year. This is undoubtedly, as I said, one of the most serious problems affecting Europe, which is the same as it is affecting our homeland, Spain, and we cannot therefore turn our backs on it. We must act shoulder to shoulder with our partners and do so within the framework of the common policies of the European Union. The third of the reasons I spoke to them was international solidarity. Ladies and gentlemen, what is happening in the Mediterranean is a tragedy, a humanitarian crisis of unusual proportions. It is estimated that 25,000 people, many of them women and children, have lost their lives swallowed by the ocean trying to reach our shores in recent years, 1,700 more in the first four months of 2015, of which 850 I remind them that they died in a single shipwreck. These figures, ladies and gentlemen, respond to the disasters we have witnessed; the real dimension of tragedy is a secret that today hides the sea. The operation for which the Government is requesting authorization today is not directed against illegal immigration; quite the contrary, it is primarily aimed at rescuing shipwrecks. This has been demonstrated by the Minister in his speech. The operation aims to put an end to those who trade in the desperation and dreams of these people, with the mafias who, taking advantage of the vacuum of power, use the coasts of Libya to overwhelm thousands of people whose fate on too many occasions is death. It intends to put an end to the criminal organizations, which have pocketed €16 billion in the last fifteen years with this inhumane and repugnant traffic. We can't give it another minute without risking our passivity becoming accomplices to that monstrosity. Finally, your ladies and gentlemen are aware that uncontrolled human trafficking between North Africa and our coasts constitutes a serious threat to Europe's collective security. Libya is today the main base of operations of jihadism in the Mediterranean area and the illegal trafficking of immigrants, the easiest method to infiltrate terrorists in the countries of the Union. I do not think the question requires further explanation. I would not like to end without making some brief reference to some of the comments I have heard. First of all, don Diego, you're such a pain in the ass. I have come to talk about the operation for the persecution of the mafias involved in the trafficking of immigrants, as well as in their rescue and, if I do not care, your lordship drags me into a debate on asylum, which you will understand is not today's debate in any case. However, as you are well aware there is a process of sharing in the European Union, we are taking part in that process and of course we will assume the outcome and the agreement that is reached, but we must not do so in haste. In any case, we will be faster than our European partners were when - and you will remember it well - we saw tens of thousands of immigrants on the coast of the Canary Islands and, however, did not come very quickly to be in solidarity with the Spanish problem. We will be faster and more supportive, do not have the slightest doubt. Don Álvaro, the first thing he has shown is that it was a ransom without return, and of course the fight against the mafias. We can't forget this. On the other hand, many of you have spoken about the problem at source. Illegal immigration in these cases, ladies and gentlemen, is a consequence of war or hunger. If your ladies and gentlemen know the solution to either of the two problems, I announce that humanity has been looking for them for approximately 10,000 years. If you could make us a piece of paper, we would be grateful for a barbarity, but in the meantime, of the 60 million political refugees referred to by Mr Tardà or of the 2 billion that will inhabit the African continent by 2040, how many do you think we should welcome in Europe? How many do you think is reasonable, 2,000, 1,500, 600 million, how many? I say this because, if we do not make that clear, talking about solidarity and all the speeches I have heard here this afternoon is very easy. But I tell you we're willing, how many do you want us to welcome, Your Honor? I have to remember that Spain is absolutely solidarity. We are in Iraq or Afghanistan precisely to contribute to the stabilization of those countries and to somehow stop that flow that we cannot control at the moment. In any case, ladies and gentlemen, I do not want to finish my speech without thanking you for the positive vote that the majority of you have shown. Thank you so much. [[Applauds]]
